=== Page 7 (pypdf) ===
7
INTRODUCTION
Government, through the Ministry of Finance , Economic Planning and
Decentralization embarked on a number of budget reforms in the areas of budget
planning, budget documentation and implementation. One of the major reforms was
the introduction of Program Based Budgeting (PBB). Program Based Budgeting
approach puts emphasis on results of p rograms and projects other than inputs or
processes. It is designed to be easily understood by the general public and also help
Parliamentarians prioritise allocation of public funds to areas that can bring results in
the various sectors.
This budget provides a simple description of the purpose and works of every ‘Vote’ (a
Ministry, Department or Agency with a budget subject to a vote of Parliament). By
looking at the Mission, Strategic Objectives and Outputs, one can easily gather the
mandate of a particular Vote. A ‘Program’ groups together activities and outputs of
a Vote which work toward a common purpose. For example, outputs of school
teachers, classrooms and textbooks all contribute toward the objective of improving
the literacy rate, therefore each of these outputs can come together under one
Program.
As the title ‘Program Based Budget’ indicates, the most important budget category is
the Program. At a glance the reader can see the resources that have been allocated
to each Program and the Program’s objectives such as improving the literacy rate or
increasing life expectancy. The funding allocated in the previous year and the
projected funding for the next two years is also included. This makes it easier for one
to see how the Government is prioritising spending.
The performance and activities of each Ministry, Department or Agency are easy to
observe in this document. For a given level of funding for a program, outcomes and
outputs are reported. Hence PBB provides both financial and performance
information. Performance indicators remain work in progress and will continue to be
improved.
Programs and projects in PBB have been linked with the Malawi 2063 and the Malawi
First 10 -Year Implementation Plan (MIP -1). Programs and outputs outlined by the
different Ministries, Department and Agencies in this budget are speaking to the key
focus areas outlined in the MIP-1.

MISCELLANEOUS AND OTHER STATUTORY PAYMENTS
Head: 020
Overview
Statutory Expenditure comprises all payments that Government is legally
obligated to make, such as compensations and other payments including
university student loans. The 2026-27 Budget Estimate is K73.4 billion.

PENSIONS AND GRATUITIES
Head: 030
Overview
Pensions and Gratuities are part of Statutory Expenditure and they include
Gratuities, Separation Benefits, Ex -gratia Payments, Death Gratuities, and
Government Contributory Pension. The 2026 -27 Budget Estimate is K302.23
billion.

STATE RESIDENCES
Vote number: 050
Controlling Officer: The Director General
I. MISSION
To serve the President of the Republic of Malawi as he delivers the National
Agenda.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To enhance institutional capacity for State House in terms of governance,
strategic leadership, structural and functional systems;
• To set for agenda for political leadership that is both responsive and
assertive; a President who is in control of the agenda; and
• To provide first -class advisory services to the State President that are
evidence-based, analytical, efficient and professional.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Rehabilitation of Kamuzu Palace in progress; Partly replaced of Furniture
and Fittings, partly painted; street lights maintained and Landscaping in
progress;
• Facilitated His Excellency’s other domestic engagements;
• Partial maintenance of irrigation system done; and
• Replaced and maintained some of damaged assets including the
Marquee Tent.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Inadequate resources to maintain all State Residences.
• Inadequate plants and Equipment in the residences.
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

NATIONAL AUDIT OFFICE
Vote number: 060
Controlling Officer: Auditor General
I. MISSION
To provide assurance on accountability, transparency, integrity and value
for money in the management of public resources to all stakeholders
through quality audits.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To increase stakeholders' confidence in government financial
management systems;
• To increase stakeholders’ confidence in MDAs compliance with
authorities;
• To improve resources utilization and service delivery in MDAs when
implementing their planned projects and programs;
• To increase stakeholders’ confidence that MDAs Information Systems
are operating effectively and efficiently;
• Increased stakeholders’ confidence in proper utilization of public
resources; and
• To improve organizational, management and administrative processes.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Financial and Compliance Audits -Conducted 110 out of 135 planned
financial and compliance audits across all government entities to ensure
adherence to financial regulations and public sector accountability
standards. This represents 81%;
• Performance Audits - Out of the 6 planned audits, NAO managed to
conduct 2, thereby, achieving 33.33% of the target;
• Information Technology/System Audits on Government Systems -
Conducted 6 out of the 8 planned audits on key government
information systems to ensure data security, reliability, and compliance
with IT governance frameworks. This represents 75% of the target
achieved;
• Review of NAO’s Strategic plan - Conducted a comprehensive review
of its 2021–2025 Strategic Plan which will inform the successor strategy to
run from 2025 to 2030; and

=== Page 28 (pypdf) ===
28
• Citizen Engagement and Gender -Sensitive Audits - Audits for three (3)
councils namely, M’mbelwa, Nkhotakota, and Machinga for the
2023/24 and three (3) councils of Magochi, Luchenza, and Kasungu for
the 2022/23 Financial Year incorporated citizen participation and
applied gender -centric approaches to ensure diverse voices are
represented, thereby enhancing public trust in the management of
public resources, and supporting improved oversight and accountability
at district council level.

OFFICE OF THE CHIEF JUSTICE
Vote number: 070
Controlling Officer: Chief Registrar
I. MISSION
To interpret, protect and enforce the Constitution and all laws in an
independent and impartial manner.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
● To promote, protect, and uphold the rule of law, democracy, human
rights and the constitution;
● To improve service delivery;
● To restore and sustain public trust;
● To enhance utilization of technology in service delivery; and
● To strengthen institutional capacity.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
● Under Child Justice the Directorate coordinated to register 1,511
cases in addition to 2,027 brought forward from the previous year
(1,463 criminal; 2,075 civil). Out of which 1,743 (716 criminal; 1,027
civil) cases were concluded. And 1,795 cases are stil l pending (747
criminal; 1,048 civil);
● The Sheriff of Malawi executed 1,451 Warrants from the 1,880 which
were issued and 429 are pending;
● Under Malawi Institute of Legal Education (MILE) out of 68 students
registered in 2024, 40 successfully completed the course and were
admitted to the bar in 2025. As for the 2025 intake, there are 115
students enrolled awaiting results in March 2026;
● Under Community Directorate, out of 179 Orders registered 88 were
completed, 42 still serving and 46 are at large;
● Developed and launched the Malawi Judiciary Strategic Plan
(MJSP) 2025–2030, Digitalization Strategy, and ICT Policy; and
● Signed a Memorandum of Understanding with the Judiciary of
Namibia
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
● Capacity gaps
● Inadequate and dilapidated infrastructure
● Shortage of motor vehicles
● Inadequate and obsolete furniture and office equipment
● Lack of proper storage facilities for court records

=== Page 36 (pypdf) ===
36
● Shortage of stock of books, space and trained officers in the library
services.

NATIONAL ASSEMBLY
Vote number: 080
Controlling Officer: Clerk of Parliament
I. MISSION
To perform representative, legislative and oversight functions for good
governance.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To represent the People of Malawi effectively;
• To discharge lawmaking and oversight functions effectively;
• To mobilise and manage financial resources effectively;
• To strengthen capacity of Members of Parliament and Staff to perform
their functions; and
• To improve and maintain the Parliament assets and infrastructure.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
The following key achievements were realised in the 2025 -2026 financial
year:
• Held Three (3) Plenary Meetings;
• Conducted Eighteen (18) Committee Meetings;
• Conducted the Swearing -in Programme for the New Members of
Parliament;
• Carried out the Orientation Programme for the new Members of
Parliament, Committees and the Political Leadership;
• Undertook the final evaluation of the 2021-2026 Strategic Plan;
• Passed the FY2025/26 Mid-Year Budget;
• Passed Six (6) Bills in line with the people’s aspirations;
• Developed the Open Government Partnerships (OGP) framework for
the 2025-2028 National Action Plan;
• Conducted Quiz competitions in three (3) districts: Balaka, Mchinji and
Rumphi; and
• Conducted street awareness programs in three (3) districts: Balaka,
Mchinji and Rumphi.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Limited office space makes it impossible to provide appropriate
accommodation to the Parliamentary Service Commission, Committee
Chairpersons, Members of Parliament, and Parliamentary staff;
• Archaic equipment which incurs huge maintenance costs and affects
the effectiveness of Service Delivery;
• Inadequate budgetary allocation, which affects the implementation of
some activities in the Strategic Plan.
• Old air conditioning, plumbing and electrical systems which require
overhauling

OFFICE OF THE DIRECTOR OF PUBLIC OFFICERS DECLARATIONS
Vote number: 081
Controlling Officer: Director of Public Officers’ Declarations
I. MISSION
To promote public confidence in the public service by receiving, verifying
and publicising public officers’ declarations, and sensitizing the public on
the declaration system.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To increase compliance in terms of timeliness, completeness and
truthfulness of declarations;
• To improve management information system for declarations;
• To improve knowledge by the general public on declarations regime;
and
• To strengthen capacity and functionality of the Directorate.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
● Received and processed 14786 declarations;
● Conducted declarations data entry exercise for the 14,786 Listed Public
Officers (LPOs);
● Conducted compliance in 208 public institutions whose findings
indicate an 80% compliance rate;
● Published compliance report for 2023/24 financial year;
● Conducted physical verification of assets, liabilities and business
interests for 38 LPOs; and
● Reviewed and formulated a new strategic plan for 2026 to 2030.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
● General rise in prices of goods and services severely affected the
implementation of the planned activities.
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE
Table 5.1: Budget by Programme and Sub-Programme (MK 000'000s)
Program/Subprogram 2025-26

public competence.

recommendations in a timely manner;

complaints system; and

OFFICE OF THE PRESIDENT AND CABINET
Vote number: 090
Controlling Officer: The Secretary to the President and Cabinet
I. MISSION
To provide strategic leadership, oversight and coordination of Public
Service to consistently achieve excellence in service delivery and
progressively provide an environment conducive for the attraction,
retention and development of its workforce.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To provide oversight, coordination and supervision on the implementation
of policies, programs and projects;
• To reprioritize public services, policies and programs and focus on impact
areas;
• To enhance results focused public service delivery;
• To improve Disaster Risk Management;
• To foster the growth of the innovation ecosystem; and
• To provide oversight and coordination on governance, general
administration and human resources management in State Owned
Enterprises (SOEs) and State Corporations (SCs).
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Serviced Cabinet and Cabinet Committee Meetings;
• Successfully conducted Public Sector Performance Evaluations for the
2024/2025 Financial Year;
• Successfully assessed 62 MDAs out of 120 MDAs;
• Conducted Public Policy reviews;
• Oriented Officials from MDAs on development of National Policy and
Cabinet Paper;
• Conducted ground-truthing exercise on SONA Commitments;
• Conducted Orientation on Individual Performance Management
System;
• Conducted Principal Secretaries Committee Meetings;
• Successful Execution of Public and National Events;
• Commenced development of a Paperless Cabinet System;
• Conducted performance monitoring of Statutory Corporations and
State-Owned Enterprises;
• Conducted performance evaluation of the Boards of Directors and
Executive Management of Statutory Corporations and State -Owned
Enterprises;
• Coordinated reconstitution of SOEs and SCs Board of Directors.
• Facilitated the operationalisation of the Offices of the Second Vice
President and Minister of State;

=== Page 68 (pypdf) ===
68
• Rehabilitated Capital Hill restaurant, Civil Service Club house, Blantyre
and Capital hill offices, and Chief Secretary official residence and guest
house;
• Procured vehicles for the Ministers and Principal Secretaries; and
• Completed compensations for Project Affected Person's at Mvela
Innovation City and Magwero Industrial Park.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Mobility challenges due to inadequate and old fleet which greatly
affected office operations.

DEPARTMENT OF HUMAN RESOURCE MANAGEMENT AND
DEVELOPMENT
Vote number: 093
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Human Resource Management and
Development
I. MISSION
To foster and sustain a high quality and result-oriented, accountable and
transparent Public Service through systematic development and
implementation of equitable, sound human and institutional resource
management policies, strategies, practices and systems in order to ensure
efficiency and effectiveness.
II. Strategic Objectives
• To provide appropriate human resource management policies, procedures
and practices in order to standardise and regulate the attraction and
retention of the public human resources;
• To develop and maintain sound and effective public service human
resources; organisational and information management systems and
structures; and
• To enhance and strengthen services through the provision of policy
direction, guidance and administrative support.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025 – 2026 FINANCIAL YEAR
• Individual Performance Management System has been revised with
support from the Chandler Institute of Governance (CIG) of Singapore;
• The Public Service Workplace Anti -Sexual Harassment Policy has been
developed.
• Conducted three (3) Leadership Development Programmes for Principal
Secretaries and Heads of Government Agencies.
• The Department developed a Strategic Plan, functional structure, staff
establishment, salary structure and conditions of service for the Malawi
School of Government as a way of managing the transition process as
well as ensuring that the school takes off smoothly.

CIVIL SERVICE COMMISSION
Vote number: 097
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Civil Service Commission
I. MISSION
To appoint qualified persons to the Civil Service based on merit and exercise fair
disciplinary control over them, for efficient and effective delivery of high quality
goods and services to the general public.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To streamline the appointment process;
• To improve the confirmation process;
• To improve the disciplinary case handling process;
• To improve the case management system for removal of persons from office; and
• To enhance operational capacity and efficiency of the institution.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/2026
• Launched the 2025-2030 Strategic Plan.
• Filled 606 vacant posts in various Ministries, Departments and Agencies
representing 90% of submissions received for both appointments and
promotions.
• Concluded 6 disciplinary cases for officers from various MDAs.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• The Civil Service Commission continues to experience financial and mobility
constraints which greatly affect implementation of its key activities. The
Commission currently relies on cost sharing with various MDAs to ably conduct
recruitment activities and training of MDAs on recruitment and disciplinary case
management.
• Heavy reliance on manual and paper -based processes continues to
contribute to delays in completion of recruitment and discipline related
activities.

MINISTRY OF DEFENCE
Vote number: 100
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Defence
I. MISSION
To regulate, promote, organize and coordinate the effective
performance of the Malawi Defence Force through the management of
the Defence Policy and monitoring of its implementation, provision of
strategic direction, management of resources, promotion of civil military
relations, facilitation of regional and international peace and order.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To provide strategic policy guidance and direction on the development
of a professional and modern Defence Force.
• To contribute to the upholding of the sovereignty, territorial integrity and
security of the Republic of Malawi.
• To contribute to political and diplomatic initiatives that promote
international peace and regional security.
• To promote internal controls and internal checks for effective
accountability of resources.
• To promote civil-military relations.
• To support the Malawi Defence Force with Infrastructure Development
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
In 2025/26 financial year, the Ministry registered the following achievements;
• Bilateral relations meeting on Joint Permanent Commission on Defence
and Security (JPCDS) with Mozambique and Zambia was held;
• Completed Water reticulation system project at Malawi Maritime Force
in Monkey-Bay.
• Achieved 92 percent completion rate for the upgrading of Cobbe
Barrack road network in Zomba.
• Started rehabilitation of soldier’s houses at Malawi Maritime Force in
Monkey-Bay.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Inadequate and dilapidated infrastructure across MDF Units

MALAWI DEFENCE FORCE
Vote Number: 101
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Defence
I. MISSION
To conduct military operations in defence of the territorial integrity,
sovereignty and constitutional order of the Republic of Malawi and its
national interests.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To uphold the sovereignty and territorial integrity of the Republic and
guard against threats to the safety of its citizens by force of arms;
• To assist the civil authorities in proper exercise of their functions;
• To uphold and protect the constitutional order in the Republic;
• To provide technical expertise and resources to assist the civilian
authorities in the maintenance of essential services on times of
emergencies; and
• To perform such other duties outside the territory of Malawi as may be
required by the Defence Force or by any treaty entered into by Malawi
in accordance with the prescriptions of the international law.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Defence security operations in support of the 2025 Tripartite Elections;
• VVIP security and ceremonial tasks;
• Intervention in support of civil authorities during disasters by Navy,
Parachute Battalion and MNS;
• Recruitment of new soldiers and commissioning officer cadets;
• Forest protection at Dzalanyama;
• Peace keeping operations in DRC as part of FIB and SAMIDRC
• Progressive training at various levels of command both local and
international;
• Conducted MDF sports festival at MAFCO;
• Engagement with Ombudsman to reduce cases related to dismissals;
• Established of a boots making factory in Blantyre;
• Procured tractors and other machinery for mordernisation of mega
farms;
• Facilitated Joint Permanent Commission for Defence and Security
(JPCDS);
• Made uniforms for recruits at Ordinance;
• Improved mobility through the procurement of 82 URAL trucks TCVs
IV. PROGRAM ISSUES
• Need to finalise formulation of the Defence Policy

=== Page 112 (pypdf) ===
112
• Need to enhance efforts to ensure that MDF is self -sustained to ably
champion operationalisation of mega farms and MDF Engineering
Company Ltd
• Finalise Functional Review process
• Research and Development. Need to enhance operational excellence
and service delivery by investing in and pursuing research, innovation and
best practices as part of the operationalisation of National Security Policy
(2018)
• Alignment of the Defence Force core and support processes with
constitutional imperatives.
• Reserves. Further continuing with the process of establishment of an active
Reserve Force to complement the Regular Force to meet defence
commitments
• Education, Training and Development (ETD) Management to be integrated
to ensure improved system of education, training, development and
capacity building in general.
• Information Systems. Need for a Defence Establishment Information System
(DEIS)to support business processes and command information to ensure
compliance, improve efficiency, security and economy.
• Strengthened relationship and communication with stakeholders.

MINISTRY OF LOCAL GOVERNMENT AND RURAL DEVELOPMENT
Vote Number: 120
Controlling Officer: The Secretary for Local Government and Rural Development.
I. MISSION
To promote and accelerate local governance, peace, and national unity,
while strengthening civic education and evidence -based policy frameworks
that support sustainable socio-economic development.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
● To provide policy and legal framework for efficient and effective operations of
the local councils;
● To promote an effective system of local governance and development;
● To promote and provide policy direction on rural development in Malawi;
● To promote socio-economic development of rural communities;
● To promote sustainable economic and physical development and growth of
cities and urban centres;
● To create a conducive environment for civic empowerment of citizens to
promote sustainable peace and unity;
● To promote, facilitate and coordinate transformative civic education and
engagement;
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
● Launched the National Decentralisation Policy;
● Supported 25 local authorities in the development, review, and interpretation
of their By-Laws;
● Facilitated the development and launch of the National Action Plan on the
United Nations Security Council Resolution 2250 on Youth, Peace, and Security;
● Developed the Civic Education Bill which aimed at regulating and coordinating
the civic space, and ensuring transformative civic education;
● Facilitated the finalization of job descriptions for devolved officers in the
information sector. This follows the approved devolution plan and management
guidelines for information and digitalization;
● Successfully celebrated Umodzi Day in Mzuzu. The celebration brought together
all Malawians from different tribes, different political blocks and religious
affiliations;
● Coordinated Local Authorities Performance Assessment (LAPA) in all 28 district
councils;
● Implemented works at Chitekesa (Phalombe) and Nambuma (Dowa) RGCs
and progress is at a 70% completion rate at both sites;
● Implemented the construction of Stadiums where the completion rate for the
four stadiums is as follows: Mzimba is at 85%, Ntcheu is at 95%, Thyolo is at 80%,
Zomba Phase 2 is at 90%, Mwanza at 22%, and Msundwe at 10%;
● Completed the construction of Thyolo DC Offices while construction of Mzimba
is at 55%;

=== Page 122 (pypdf) ===
122
● Construction of Mzuzu Civic Office is on-going and progress is at 70 percent
completion rate;
● Completed the construction of chiefs’ house with 8 near completion, and 20
at window level;
● Construction of Gwanda Chakuamba Mausoleum and Orton Chirwa
Mausoleum. The completion rate for Orton Chirwa Mausoleum is at 22% while
Gwanda Chwakuamba works have just commenced;
● Issued 97 licenses for public entertainment facilities, 38 permits for public
entertainment, and classified 97 films for exhibition; and
● Held the National Conference for the Creative and and Natural Resources,
Fisheries, Information, Tourism, Industry, Mining, and Energy.
● Supported seven secondary cities in the preparation of their Master plans,
namely: Monkey Bay, Liwonde, Karonga, Chipoka, Nkhatabay and Kasungu.
● Reviewed all the Local Government System Handbooks that guide the
functioning of Local Government Authorities in Malawi;
● Identified and supported the operationalization of the Mwanza Local Authority
Revenue Management Information System (LoRMIS) under Public Sector
Reforms and the Integrated National Financing Framework (INFF).
● Coordinated and facilitated the signing of the agreement with the Embassy of
Iceland for the Mangochi Basic Service Programme Phase III (MBSP III), as well
as the initiation of the third Iceland Partnership with three district councils.
● Facilitated the implementation of the TRADE Programme, which is being
implemented in 11 districts and aims to increase value chain commercialisation
and enhance the resilience of rural poor and smallholder producers.
● Introduced the Decentralisation Lab to act as a platform of engagement with
MDAs to identify gaps and challenges affecting decentralization.
● Operationalised Karonga Town Council.
● Conducted Local Authority Performance Assessment (LAPA) in all the 28 district
councils which formed the basis for disbursing performance-based Grants (PBG)
under the GESD and RCRP.
● Coordinated the review of the National Peace Policy (2017) to ensure
continued relevance and alignment with emerging social, political, and
climate-related peace and security dynamics.
● Facilitated the development of the Integrated National Mindset Change
Programme, aimed at harmonising mindset change initiatives and promoting
positive values, productivity, integrity, and responsible citizenship.
● Established community dialogue hubs (Pabwalo) at both district and Traditional
Authority levels, creating structured platforms for civic engagement, dialogue,
and local problem-solving.
● Facilitated the development of the National Peace Architecture Monitoring
and Evaluation Framework to operationalise and institutionalise evidence -
based decision-making, strengthen accountability, and enhance conflict early-
warning and response mechanisms.
● Established two District Peace and Unity Committee adding to a total of eleven,
strengthening local -level conflict prevention, mediation, and peacebuilding
capacity.
IV. PROGRAM ISSUES
● Implementation of activities was affected due to lower than planned
disbursement of resources;
● Delays in payments at the Accountant General's Office;

=== Page 123 (pypdf) ===
123
● Week enforcement of policies and regulations compounded by the slow
formulation of council bylaws undermine d efforts to enhance service delivery
and improve revenue collection;
● Forex shortage delayed procurement of critical equipment which delayed the
implementation of some projects;
● Prolonged approval process by Office of the President and Cabinet, Public
Procurement and Disposal of Assets Authority , Ministry of Justice, and the
National Gender Unit (NGU) adversely affects the implementation of planned
activities;
● The lack of adequate vehicles, furniture, and sufficient office space —
compromises the effective delivery of services;
● Persistence of chieftaincy disputes due to non -compliance with established
succession plans, traditions, and customs; and
● Inadequate workforce to meet organizational needs.

NATIONAL LOCAL GOVERNMENT FINANCE COMMITTEE
Vote number: 121
Controlling Officer: Executive Director
I. MISSION
To provide technical, financial and economic management services in an
efficient and effective manner with the view of ensuring adequate
mobilization, equitable distribution and efficient utilization of resources for
the delivery of quality demand driven services by Local Governments.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To improve mobilization, allocation and utilization of public resources by
Local Governments;
• To improve accountability and transparency in the management of
public resources by Local Governments;
• To enhance regulatory compliance, efficiency and effectiveness in the
use of public resources by Local Governments;
• To support planning and management of development resources at
Local Authority and community levels; and
• To facilitate the implementation of the Integrated Rural Development
Strategy.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Reviewed the 2024/25 councils’ financial statements and submitted to
the Auditor General for audit in line with the regulatory requirements;
• Facilitated stakeholder validation of the revised Intergovernmental
Fiscal Transfer Formulae; and Local Authority Financing and Fiscal
Decentralization Strategy;
• Facilitated implementation of 101 infrastructural projects financed
through Performance Based Grant (PBG) under Governance to Enable
Service Delivery (GESD) Project in all 28 district councils;
• Facilitated implementation of 83 infrastructural projects financed
through Performance Based Grant (PBG) under Regional Climate
Resilience Programme (RCRP-2) in all 28 district councils;
• Supported 242,067 ultra-poor and labour constrained households under
the Social Cash Transfer Programme (SCTP) in 21 districts representing
89% of the target;
• Supported 487,894 ultra-poor households under the Climate Smart
Enhanced Public Works Programme (CS -EPWP) in 28 districts
representing 94% of the target;
• Supported 31,686 households in Blantyre and Chiradzulu under Scalable
Safety Nets Programme. A total of MK3.17 billion was disbursed; and
each household received MK50,000 per month for two months to offset
drought-related income losses; and

=== Page 138 (pypdf) ===
138
• Supported 3,777 youths with start -up tools under the Youth Skills
Challenge Initiative through the Livelihoods Support Programme in 28
district councils.
IV. PROGRAM ISSUES
• Some councils had been slow in accounting for the first tranche of 60%
of PBG 3.1 resources hence delaying disbursement of the remaining 40%
of the resources. This contributed to further delays in implementation of
the projects. Problematic councils are be ing targeted with tailor made
technical support to avoid future recurrence of the same issue.
• Delays in synchronizing Mthandizi, Unified Beneficiary Registry (UBR), and
Payment Service Provider (PSP) data resulted in mismatched payrolls
and unverified updates in several councils. The NLGFC will fast -track full
API integration between Mthandizi and the UBR and assign dedicated
ICT officers per region.
• Mobile money agents in Nsanje, Mwanza and Chikwawa experienced
float shortages and unresolved Know Your Customer (KYC) verification
failures. Coordination with PSPs will be strengthened on agent float
management and national KYC clean -up will be completed
accordingly.
• Price escalations/inflationary effects on the contracts which have been
implemented over a long period of time had led to reduced PBG project
scopes in some councils. Other councils have planned to complete the
works using PBG 4.0 resources while in other cases other funding sources
will be explored to complete the works.
• The process of review and approval of Environmental and Social
Management Plans (ESMPs) for projects under Regional Climate Resilient
Programme (RCRP-2) had been slow hence affecting roll out of cycle 1
projects. The NLGFC is engaging the World Bank to put in place an
effective mechanism that would expedite review and approval of
ESMPs for projects in the subsequent cycles.

MINISTRY OF LANDS, HOUSING AND URBAN DEVELOPMENT
Vote number: 130
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Lands, Housing and Urban Development
I. MISSION
To create an enabling environment for efficient, effective and sustainable
provision of land and housing services to the general public in order to
promote and encourage sustainable development
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To improve access to land and security of land tenure for socio -economic
development;
• To improve land use planning and management for orderly and
coordinated physical development;
• To improve provision of geospatial information for social economic
development;
• To increase access to affordable, safe and secure housing for all income
groups; and
• To strengthen institutional capacity for efficient and effective
implementation of the land, housing and urban development programmes.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Completed the 2.7 km Katoto ADMARC access road in Mzuz u while
2 access roads in Lilongwe Area 32 and Area 43 are at 99 percent
and 78 percent completion respectively;
• Established and trained 50 Customary Land Committees, 73
Customary Land Tribunals, 4 District Land Tribunals and the Central
Land Board (CLB)
• 108 staff houses have been completed and officially handed over to
the Malawi Police Service . An additional 64 houses have been
completed and are pending handover, comprising of 40 houses for
the Malawi Defence Force and 24 houses for the Malawi Police
Service. Construction of 407 houses are at an average of 85%
completion;185 houses are at 74% completion; 142 houses are at 59%
completion and 1,438 houses are below 50%.
• Produced a draft Urban Structure Plan for Chipoka Secondary City.
• The Ministry successfully identified land to resettle the displaced
people at Nthunduwala camp in Kasungu
• Conducted a Joint Survey with Zambia. The exercise validated 122
areas affected by the boundary reaffirmation and demarcation
works that were conducted for whole boundary stretch of 980km
from Coumba Tri -Union Pillar in Mchinji/Vubwe Districts to Nakonde
Tri-Union Pillar in Chitipa/Nakonde District of Malawi and Zambia
respectively from 1980 to 2025.

• Developed 34 Village Land Use Plans in different group village
authority across the country to guide orderly physical developments
in villages and also as a prerequisite for land titling.
• Established 35 Help Desk Offices in local councils across the country
to assist the public in accessing land related services.
• 29,936 customary estates were successfully registered under LIMS,
while 319 transactions were recorded in the Deeds Registry and 672
transactions were registered in the Land Registry.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Inadequate specialized equipment in technical departments including
Global Positioning System (GPS) for surveying;
• Delays i n procurement and payments processes contributes to slow
progress and inefficiencies in implementation of work plan activities;
• Lengthy procurement procedures which affect implementation of
projects; and
• Mobility challenges due to shortage of vehicles.

MINISTRY OF YOUTH, SPORTS AND CULTURE
Vote number: 180
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Youth, Sports and Culture
I. MISSION
To deliver inclusive youth, sports, and cultural programs that enhance
opportunities, strengthen institutions, preserve heritage, and promote
creative expression for sustainable development.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
1. To enhance the well-being, skills, employability, and civic participation
of youth in national development.
2. To e xpand access to safe, inclusive sports and recreation, improving
athlete development and integrity through better governance
3. To develop arts and cultural industries to boost cultural expression, social
cohesion, and economic growth.
4. To s afeguard and promote Malawi’s cultural heritage for education,
tourism, and intergenerational transmission.
5. To strengthen archives and records management for improved access
to information and accountability in the public sector
6. To e nhance institutional capacity and resource mobilisation to
effectively deliver the Ministry’s mandate.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Mzuzu Youth Centre construction procject progressed up 30% level;
• Provided training and handover start up to 54 NYS Cohort 2;
• Developed MoUs with the following organizations: CAMFED Malawi on
Girls’ Education and Empowerment; Grassroots Soccer Malawi on
Mental Health Programming, Tonny Elumelo Foundation on Youth
Enterprise Development, and Centre on Human Rights Research and
Outreach on Human Rights and Youth Engagement;
• Disseminated the National Youth Policy to all districts except for Likoma
District;
• Conducted the Commemoration of International Youth Day;
• Resumed construction of the Soche Stadium in Blantyre;
• Hosted the African Netball Cup in December 2025;
• Completed the construction of an Information Centre at Chongoni
Rock-Art World Heritage Area in Dedza District has been completed,
and the centre was officially opened and is currently in use;
• introduced an innovative reform of digitising public records; and
• Enhanced participation in cultural exchange programs and
international events.

MINISTRY OF AGRICULTURE, IRRIGATION AND WATER DEVELOPMENT
Vote number: 190
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Agriculture Irrigation and Water
Development
I. MISSION
To improve agricultural productivity and sustainably develop and manage
land and water resources to achieve food, nutrition and income security for
economic growth and development
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
● To ensure sustained food security;
● To ensure increased agro-based incomes;
● To ensure sustainable land management; and
● To increase area under irrigation farming.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
Agriculture Sector
● Established and supported 13,802 Farmer Field Schools with
participation of 303,644 (97,167 Males, 206477 Females) farmers
● Facilitated revamping of DAESS Structures at all levels in all districts.
● Increased adoption rate of improved technologies from 30% to 46% for
example, sustainable land management
● Reached 1,360,809 farmers through multimedia campaigns on
nutrition, post-harvest handling, irrigation, catchment management
and pest management
● Promoted Gender Equality and social Inclusion
● Supported 16,211 Income Generating Groups
● 56840 households are implementing Household Approach.
● Development of Gender and Targeting strategies for SAPP and PRIDE
projects.
● Served more than 250,000 farmers through digital extension and
advisory services through Malawi Digital Plant Health Services
(MADIPHS) and Ulimi ndi Nyengo SMS system.
● Produced and aired 225 radio and 44 TV programs
● Facilitated development of knowledge management and
communication strategy documents
● Increased population of major livestock species
o Cattle: increased by 2.76% (from 2,269,055 to 2,331,687)
o Goats: increased by 5.0% (from 14,090,773 to 14,818,891)
o Sheep: increased by 5.9% (from 459,959 to 487,265)
o Pigs: increased by 8.9% (from 12,475,657 to 13,581,236)
o Indigenous chickens: increased by 5.8% (from 113,860,549 to
120,490,248)

=== Page 172 (pypdf) ===
172
● Re-opened Mikolongwe Hatchery in 2025 and now has started selling 6
weeks chicks
● sold 47,207 Black Australorp chicks to smallholder poultry farmers
● Provided artificial insemination services, over 85,000 cows inseminated
with local and imported semen
● Facilitated Importation of 90 pure Jersey cattle at Dwambazi for
breeding
● Prevented outbreaks of FMD in all high risk areas by vaccinating 51,293
cattle against Foot and Mouth Disease (FMD)
● Vaccinated 342,702 dogs and cats against rabies
● Developed disease control strategies for FMD, Newcastle Disease,
Peste des Petits Ruminants (PPR), Highly Pathogenic Avian Influenza
(HPAI), African Swine Fever (ASF) and Rabies
● Increased I-2 vaccine production Capacity from 10,000,000 to
30,000,000 doses per year
● Constructed 6 greenhouses against target of 8 with another 6 already
procured waiting for supplier to construct.
● Provided mechanisation hiring to over 1021farmers with over 1465 ha
(ploughed, harrowed & ridged) and 1319 metric tons of maize shelled
● Procured and distributed 6 tractors, 4 ploughs, 4 ridgers, 6 harrows, 6
multi-crop row planters and 6 multi-crop threshers to all the ADDs
● Trained 32 mechanics and 31 Farm Mechanisation officers on
operation, servicing and maintenance of tractor engines
● Revised mechanisation services hiring rates which were stagnant for
over 3-4 years despite rising scheme operational costs
● 200 catchment maps depiction land cover, slope, elevation and
spatial locations of land degradation hotspots
● 369 staff members and 1, 283,058 farmers trained on sustainable land
and water management technologies
● 2,675,516 agroforestry tree seedlings were raised covering about
800,00ha
Water Sector
● 9 Ground Water reticulated systems developed under MWASIP
● 65 Ground Water sources developed
● 7 Flood monitoring and focusing stations established
● 4 sensitization meetings on managed aquifer recharge undertaken
● 13 ground water sources rehabilitated
● 38 Afridev Boreholes constructed
● 5 Solar powered high yielding boreholes completed.
● 214 boreholes completed in health care facilities under global fund
● 1 operation and maintenance Model facilitated
● 1 Design manual for climate resilient infrastructure developed
● 1 Gravity fed Scheme at Nyika Vwaza constructed
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
● Limited access to ORT funds for implementation of activities
● Inadequate motorcycles for Front line staff mobility
● Dilapidated office and housing facilities

=== Page 173 (pypdf) ===
173
● Inadequate resources to support on farm demonstrations (OFDs)
● Limited and unreliable mobility for extension and veterinary staff
● Dilapidated infrastructure and obsolete equipment, including
laboratories, farm machinery, and production facilities, limiting
effective service delivery
● Frequent incapacitation of water resource infrastructures and water
supply systems by disasters
● Vandalism/theft of borehole-pump parts and piped water systems
infrastructure (e.g. submersible pumps)
● Inefficient management of rural water supply systems.
● Substandard works by some contractors.
● Absence of an independent regulator for the water supply and
sanitation sector.
● Shortage of adequate human resource at national and district council
levels.
● High levels of non-revenue water for piped water supply schemes,
especially the Water Boards.
● Forex shortage which affected importation of materials for
implementation of works.
● Devaluation of the kwacha which increased cost of materials
● Unavailability of allocated resources for the implementation of the
Resettlement Action Plan (RAP).
● Erosion of project value due to foreign exchange losses, especially on
local contracts denominated in foreign currencies but paid in Malawi
Kwacha

MINISTRY OF WATER AND SANITATION
Vote number: 210
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Water and Sanitation
I. MISSION
To manage and develop water resources for the sustainable, effective, and efficient
provision of safe water and sanitation in support of the Malawi 2063.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To improve water resources management for socio-economic growth and
development;
• To increase availability and accessibility of water supply for socio-economic
growth and development;
• To increase universal access to Improved Sanitation and Safe Hygiene
practices; while ensuring Sustainable Management of the Environment for
Socio-economic Growth and Development; and
• To improve sector management for the efficient provision of water and
sanitation.
•
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2024/25
• Drilled 50 boreholes of which 39 were wet serving a population of around
200,000
• Launched National Sanitation and Hygiene Week at Chinsapo UNICEF Ground
in Lilongwe, which saw the implementation of sanitation and hygiene activities
in over 15 districts
• Conducted Menstrual Hygiene Day, Global Handwashing Day, and World
Toilet Day commemoration events Pinda Primary School Ground, Mchinji,
Chinsapo UNICEF Ground, Lilongwe, and at Top Ten Football Ground, Salima,
respectively
• Conducted and monitored Clean-up Campaigns in 4 city and 21 district
councils
• Expanded sewer network for Kauma Sewage Treatment Plant, increasing
connection to 4,710 customers against a target of 5000 under the Lilong we
Water and Sanitation Project
• Constructed additional household latrines and improved/upgraded additional
latrines with SATO pan s, raising the reach to 4,000 and 30,000, respectively,
under the Lilongwe Water and Sanitation Project.
• Constructed additional Sanitation facilities in Markets and Schools of Lilongwe
under the Lilongwe Water and Sanitation project, making a total of 22.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Delayed and intermittent monthly ORT Funding;
• Delays in processing payments after disbursement of funds;
• Monthly allocation is inadequate for effective implementation of activities
• Devaluation of the Malawi Kwacha influencing price increase of borehole
drilling and materials

=== Page 200 (pypdf) ===
200
• Old fleet of Drilling Vehicles and Equipment leading to frequent breakdowns

OFFICE OF THE VICE PRESIDENT
Vote Number: 240
Controlling Officer: Secretary to the Vice President and Public Sector Reforms
I. MISSION
To effectively coordinate the implementation of conferred and delegated
functions through use of the assigned directorates and provide strategic
leadership in Public Sector for efficient public service delivery for the well-
being of Malawians.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To coordinate, facilitate all public functions as well as provide technical
and professional advice to the Vice President;
• To improve institutional capacity and organizational effectiveness; and
• To foster linkages with other Government institutions, the private sector,
civil society organisations and members of the general public.
• To enhance and improve planning, coordination, and implementation
of Public Sector Reforms across a ll MDAs for effective public service
delivery.
• To propagate and institutionalize Public Sector Reforms Agenda for
improved Public Sector Delivery.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Effectively facilitated and coordinated official engagements for the
Office of the Vice President;
• Enhanced Bilateral and Multilateral relations under the Presidency;
• Faciliated all delegated functions by coordinating DoDMA and PSRM
programmes;
• Commenced rehabilitation of the Area 12 residence in Lilongwe;
• Maintained CCTV system to enhance security at the Office.
• Monitored and facilitated the review of quarterly public sector reforms
progress reports across all public institutions and provided technical
guidance on implementation;
• Developed the Public Sector Innovation Strategy in collaboration with
the Department of Innovation and Creativity for integration into the
National Science, Technology and Innovation Policy framework;
• Developed and operationalised key modules of the Public Sector
Reforms Information Management System (PSRIMS), including the MDAs
Portal and Secretariat Portal, to support digital planning, monitoring, and
reporting of reforms;
• Oriented MDAs on the Reforms Planning, Implementation, Monitoring
and Evaluation Tool, to improve performance tracking and internal
performance management;
• Laid the foundation for full digital administration of the 2026/27 –2027/28
reforms contract cycle through PSRIMS to enhance efficiency,

=== Page 208 (pypdf) ===
208
transparency, and results-based reforms management.

OFFICE OF THE SECOND VICE PRESIDENT
Vote Number: 241
Controlling Officer: Secretary to the Second Vice President
I. MISSION
To effectively coordinate the implementation of conferred and
delegated functions through use of the assigned directorates to
contribute to public service delivery for the well-being of Malawians.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To coordinate and facilitate all public functions of the Second Vice
President- improve service delivery on delegated functions;
• To maintain the general welfare of the Second Vice President;
• To provide technical and professional advice to the Second Vice
President on a wide range of issues and public management issues;
• To improve institutional capacity and organizational effectiveness; and
• To foster linkages with other Government institutions, the private sector,
civil society organisations and members of the general public.

MINISTRY OF EDUCATION, SCIENCE AND TECHNOLOGY
Vote number: 250
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Education, Science and Technology
I. MISSION
To provide quality and relevant education to the Malawian Nation.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To ensure all children have access to, and complete, free and quality
education;
• To provide students with the knowledge, skills, and adaptability to
enable them to earn a living, contribute to national development and
survive in an employment constrained environment;
• To improve equitable access to quality education in universities and
higher education institutions; and
• To improve quality of management, administration, and education
planning to ensure full compliance to education policies and delivery of
quality education services.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• The Ministry implemented tablet -based learning for standard 1 to 4
students in 1,089 primary schools in all the 34 education districts through
the Building Education Foundation through Innovation Technology
(BEFIT) Programme;
• 689,762 learners (345,492 boys and 344,270 girls) have access to
technology-supported literacy and numeracy learning nationwide;
• 17,900 primary school teachers have been trained in the delivery of
tablet-enabled learning;
• 660 new jobs created supporting local economic empowerment and
strengthening programme operations at community and school levels;
• 1,000,000 community members, including traditional and local leaders
have been sensitized, thereby enhancing community ownership,
security awareness and support for programme activities under the
tablet based learning;
• Constructed 2,158 classrooms and 321 sanitation blocks under MERP;
• Engaged and placed 3,693 auxiliary teachers (ATs) to schools with high
pupil qualified teacher ratio (PqTR), in the lower primary;
• Implemented the Reformed Hardship Support Scheme (RHSS) in 1,537
hard- to- reach schools benefitting 15,427 primary school teachers who
received additional MK34,000 monthly, on top of the existing Rural
Allowance Scheme (RAS);
• Constructed 52 female teachers’ houses (20 semi -detached and 12
detached) in all the twenty-eight (28) districts targeting the rural areas is
underway and at different levels of completion;

=== Page 222 (pypdf) ===
222
• Trained 10,200 Primary school Headteachers and Deputy Headteachers,
Quality Assurance Officers and inspectors in basic school leadership and
management.
• Commenced the development of the new curriculum for Pre -primary,
primary, secondary and teacher education;
• Rolled out the National Mathematics Curriculum Reforms programme for
Stds 3 and 4 in all primary schools in Malawi and 30,000 teachers and 730
Specialist teachers were trained to implement the reform;
• 250,000 assorted textbooks have been procured for the senior primary
section to reduce pupil text book ratio;
• With financial support from UNICEF, through the KIND project, the Ministry
procured 22,831 two -seater desks and these have been delivered to
schools across the country;
• Abolished all forms of fees in all public primary and secondary schools;
• Recruited 20,571 primary school teachers which were equitably
distributed to all the 34 Education Districts;
• 2 Model Resource Centres for inclusive education are under
construction in Balaka and Mchinji;
• 600 teachers have been trained in basic sign language across the
country and 52 sign language clubs formed in schools;
• 968 specialist teachers trained in Inclusive Education pedagogy and
management of resource centres;
• 1,116 (605 males and 511 females) students with special needs were
selected into Secondary education;
• Procured and distributed computers, Orbit Readers, Braille papers,
Otoscopes, wheelchairs, embossing paper, TV Screens, Audiometers,
Sign language Dictionaries, Hearing Aids for hard of hearing students,
100 sewing machines, 100 knitting machines and 100 c arpentry tool
boxes to facilitate inclusive education;
• Commenced the expansion of 40 secondary schools of which 35 are
community day secondary schools in April 2025 and progress ranges
from 20% to 27%; and
• 72 secondary school teachers across the country were trained in
Technical Education and 54 teachers were trained in Clothing & Textiles.
• Under EQUALS, expansion of 70 CDSSs through construction of classroom
blocks, twin science laboratory blocks, ICT/ library blocks and pit latrines
are at 90% completion rate;
• The construction of 95 cost effective classrooms is at 95% completion
rate and seventy -nine (79) classroom blocks completed and handed
over for use under EQUALS;
• Expanded access to virtual science laboratories (MiLab) for Science,
Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM) simulations and
analytical sciences in partnership with Mzuzu University to additional 51
Community Day Secondary Schools (CDSSs);
• Rehabilitated the Malawi College of Distance Education (MCDE) central
region offices which will serve as an EdTech hub;
• Construction of an Entrepreneur Training and Incubation Center (ETIC)
at MZUNI is at a completion rate of 37%;

=== Page 223 (pypdf) ===
223
• Construction of a Teaching Complex hosting 4 Classrooms, 2 Science
labs, and an ODeL space at KUHeS- Lilongwe Campus is at a completion
rate of 46%;
• Construction of an integrated Teaching Facility and Innovation Hub that
incorporates a Dental School and Teaching & Research laboratories at
KUHeS- BT Campus is at a completion rate of 18%;
• Construction of a Fashion and Design Teaching & Learning Complex at
NRC Campus is at a completion rate of 60%;
• Construction of a Food Processing Centre at Bunda Campus is at a 10%
completion rate;
• Construction of a two -story ODeL building hosting 4 classrooms,
1laboratory, 1 workshop,1 design studio and e -library at MUBAS - Nanjiri
Site in Lilongwe is at completion rate of 8%;
• Construction of 3 Lecture Theatres at MUST with a capacity of 250 for a
large one and 125 for the other two is at a completion rate of 28%;
• Construction of an Open Distance and e -Learning (ODeL) Hub at
Domasi College of Education is at a Completion rate of 30%;
• Completed the construction of a 400 -student capacity premier
practical training facility for tourism and hospital management at MZUNI
has been completed;
• Construction of a 5000 -capacity three-story modern MZUNI Library and
a 500-capacity Auditorium is at 59% completion rate;
• Construction of UNIMA Administration Complex is at a completion rate
of 95%;
• Construction of 4 prefabricated classrooms at UNIMA to accommodate
2000 (500 x 4) in an hour at 70% completion rate;
• Construction of KUHeS Administration complex at Blantyre Campus that
includes a 600 -capacity graduation hall, 40 offices, 7 meeting rooms,
staff cafeteria & an in-house clinic for staff and students at a completion
rate of 46%;
• Construction of an Industrial Park at MUST is at a completion rate of 65%.
• Increased support to university students, with loan beneficiaries rising
from 31,000 in the 2024/25 financial year to 33,000 in 2025/26. The total
amount disbursed has also grown significantly, from MK29.4 billion to
MK36 billion over the same period;
• Developed innovative technologies through Nalikule College of
Education which include Automotive Waste Oil Stove, National Disaster
Alert System, Online Voting System, and an Online
Shopping/Marketplace System;
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• There are still large disparities in deployment of teachers among and
within schools, and rural allowances have not been effective;
• Mobility challenges;
• Most CDSSs do not have laboratories and libraries despite the roll out of
the new curriculum;
• Shortage of teaching space in both primary and secondary schools;

=== Page 224 (pypdf) ===
224
• Shortage of science teachers;
• Shortage of Teaching and Learning Materials;
• Enrolment is below target at secondary level because of limited intake
capacity;
• Lower pass rate for girls at MSCE;
• Shortage of teaching space;
• Low female to male ratios at tertiary level;
• Very few students with disabilities enroll in higher education institutions
due to the fact that majority of the infrastructure in universities do not
cater for the needs of people with disabilities;
• Enrolment in areas critical for economic development such as
engineering, business, ICT, and tourism is relatively low due to lack of
appropriate facilities; and
• Low equitable access to quality Higher Education.
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

MININISTRY OF HIGHER EDUCATION
Vote number: 251
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Higher Education
I. MISSION
• To provide quality and relevant education to the Malawian Nation
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To provide students with the knowledge, skills, and adaptability to
enable them to earn a living, contribute to national development and
survive in an employment constrained environment;
• To improve equitable access to quality education in universities and
higher education institutions; and
• To improve quality of management, administration, and education
planning to ensure full compliance to education policies and delivery of
quality education services.
III. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Shortage of Teaching and Learning Materials;
• Shortage of teaching space;
• Low female to male ratios;
• Very few students with disabilities enroll in higher education institutions
due to the fact that majority of the infrastructure in universities do not
cater for the needs of people with disabilities; and
• Enrolment in areas critical for economic development such as
engineering, business, ICT, and tourism is relatively low due to
underdeveloped ICT and internet facilities, and inadequate well -
equipped laboratories and classroom infrastructure.
IV. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

MINISTRY OF FOREIGN AFFAIRS AND INTERNATIONAL COOPERATION
Vote number: 260
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Foreign Affairs
I. MISSION
To promote and protect Malawi’s national interest through
implementation of its Foreign Policy, in order to contribute to sustainable
socio-economic development.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To promote Sound Political and Social Relations;
• To Establish, Maintain and Promote International Cooperation; and
• To Provide State and Government Protocol and Consular Services.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Catalysed economic opportunities by facilitating the Malawi -Israel
Labour Agreement and the Macadamia Nut Export Agreement with
China, opening new revenue streams and employment avenues.
• Successfully leveraged improved bilateral relations to facilitate the
launch of the “Advancing Climate -Resilience and Transformation in
African Coffee (ACT Coffee)” initiative, funded by the Government of
Italy. This project directly enhances the resilienc e and value of a key
Malawian export commodity.
• Actively negotiated trade and investment agreements with non -
traditional partners across Europe and beyond. This strategic shift is
designed to reduce dependency, increase the volume and value of
Malawi’s trade, and attract diversified Foreign Direct Inves tment (FDI)
into new sectors
• Enhanced the Mega Farm Initiative through a USD 300,000 donation of
agricultural machinery from Korea.
• Malawi-Morocco Strategic Partnership:
o Secured concrete commitments through ministerial consultations,
including a US$1,000,000 disaster relief grant, 1,000 MT of fertilizer
grant, 500,000 MT of subsidized commercial fertilizer, support for a
domestic blending factory, and increased scholarship slots. This
directly supports economic recovery and human capital
development.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Delay in remitting funding at reserve Bank of Malawi to Missions including
foreign service allowances, operation resources and funding for recalls
of diplomats.
• The International Cooperation program operates under pressure with
limited resources as provided by the budget ceiling to deliver all
activities.

MINISTRY OF FINANCE, ECONOMIC PLANNING AND
DECENTRALIZATION
Vote number: 270
Controlling Officer: Secretary to the Treasury
I. MISSION
To formulate robust economic, fiscal and monetary policies, mobilise and
prudently manage public financial and material resources and to provide
professional, social, economic and development services for sustainable
economic growth and development.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To improve public financial management;
• To improve resources mobilisation;
• To develop stable macroeconomic environment conducive for sustainable
socio-economic development;
• To promote a vibrant financial sector that supports inclusive economic
growth; and
• To improve institutional capacity as well as the Ministry’s organisational
efficiency and effectiveness.
• To improve economic policy formulation, planning and analysis;
• To improve development planning, programming and coordination; and
• To improve and sustain the national system for the monitoring and
evaluation of
development policies, programmes and strategies.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Conducted compliance and follow up audit in 35 and 38 parastatals
respectively.
• Developed investigative manual for the Department of Internal Auditing
• Conducted Quality assurance in 22 Local Councils
• Migration of bank accounts for SOEs to Reserve Bank of Malawi
• Formulated the 2026/27 National Budget
• Conducted the 2025/26 mid-year budget review
• Conducted a Business Interviews Survey to collect macroeconomic
data from the business community for compiling the National Accounts
and Balance of Payments;
• Produced the Economic and Fiscal Policy Statement for 2025/26
• Conducted a Business Perception and Inflation Expectation Survey to
engage with the business communities about the performance of
economy and expectations in the medium term;
• Produced Monthly Fiscal Tables to guide annual budget Implementation

=== Page 272 (pypdf) ===
272
• Conducted Quarterly Macro-Economic Analysis
• Produced two economic policy research paper on procurement and
levies procure
• Successfully undertook Audit Assignments of CHAM, Police and five
district Councils
• Conducted Quality assurance improvement programme in 4 MDAs
• Produced Public Domestic Debt Issuance Calendars to guide borrowing
• Conducted PSIP project appraisals for 360 project submissions by MDAs;
• Filled the position of Auditor General;
• Recruited Revenue Tribunal officers
• Conducted Business Interview surveys and updated GDP Framework;
• Prepared the 2023 Malawi Economic Outlook;
• Conducted project appraisals for all PSIP projects submitted by MDAs;
• Undertook joint field validation on selected PSIP projects;
• Defined the National Harmonised Management Information System (HN-
MIS) functional requirements as part of the process of supporting HN-MIS
development and deployment;
• Conducted vulnerability assessment and analysis and provided food
security updates;
• Prepared the draft Malawi National Social Protection Strategy;
IV. PROGRAM ISSUES
• High vacancy rate at senior levels in the Internal Audit Common Service
• Failure or delayed implementation of audit recommendations by MDAs
• Non – compliance of Ministries, Departments and Agencies (MDA) to
submit quarterly progress reports;
• Inadequate advanced capacity building and professional
development programmes at all levels of technical staff to enable
efficient and effective undertaking of specialized and complex
economic analyses to adequately inform design, implementation and
evaluation of develop policies and macro-economic modelling;
• Need for more concerted efforts to ensure that sector policies, plans and
strategies are aligned to the Malawi 20263 and it first 10year
implementation plan (MIP -1) as the implementation period for MIP -1 is

ACCOUNTANT GENERAL’S DEPARTMENT
Vote number: 271
Controlling Officer: The Accountant General
I. MISSION
• To design, develop, and provide high quality financial management
systems and services that are compliant with national and international
standards to public sector, by using modern technology so as to
facilitate dissemination of timely, accurate, reliable and relevant
financial management information to clients at all times.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To provide high-quality financial management and accounting services
in line with national and international standards and best practices;
• To provide efficient and effective pay services;
• To provide of efficient Information and Communication Technology
(ICT) services to the Department.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Prepared vote and consolidated financial statements on IPSAS accrual
stage 3 (modified accrual) using the system generated trail balance as
opposed to using other reports which are on cash basis.
• Trained external and internal auditors on IPSAS Certification (48 in total
with 100% certification)
• Implemented workflow for Funds Management, Accounts Payable,
Materials Management to automate processes enabling better
collaboration of tasks within the SAP IFMIS to improve efficiency and
timely completion of tasks;
• Trained and rolled out Contract Management functionality in fifteen (15)
MDAS to enhance management, recording and tracking of government
contracts in MDAs;
• Conducted system re -configurations to align with IPSAS Accrual
Accounting.
• Conducted the business functional testing (BFT) for the GoM360 system
and the Platform was recommended for rolling out to stakeholders.
• Migrated all Regulatory Authorities bank accounts to RBM, currently,
remaining with commercial parastatals
• Performed maintenance of User Accounts. Roles and Authorisations in
SAP in order to re-align system privileges according to user levels.
• Piloted Biometric System for IFMIS to add another level for
authentication.
• Reviewed Salary, Pension and EFT Payment systems.
• Commencement of land and buildings valuation.

=== Page 288 (pypdf) ===
288
• Undertaken an assessment of MDAs’ readiness for digitalized systems
which identified institutional and technical gaps for IFMIS revenue
module use & Payment Gateway use.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Approved and revised revenue budgets still not captured in the SAP
IFMIS even after consultations with relevant stakeholders.
• Limited/inadequate network (GWAN) bandwidth hampering
implementation of some SAP functionalities like FIORI and
operationalization of Disaster Recovery site;
• Intermittent network connectivity affecting system availability;
• No budget for the GOM360 project, especially for the configuration of
the diverse accounting systems for the stakeholders, UATs and trainings.
(the initial activities were supported by IFMIS)
• Development of the required files on GoM360, requires substantial
investment/budget i.e. pay the system vendors for the development of
the files or in some cases upgrading the systems.
• The process of migrating Commercial Parastatals is slow due to complex
bank structures. In most cases each parastatal has a unique structure
according to their unique function thus implementation is not one size
fits all.
• Most revenue collection points are still manual leaving the need to
automate revenue those collection points to achieve most benefits
from the TSA.
• The TSA project is not adequately funded to complete the
studies/research around the accounts consolidation and to train all
SOEs and Projects part I on the implementation of section 94 and 45 of
the PFMA.
• The manual reconciliation on MG1 and the foreign payment module
configuration readiness affect the quality and timely submission of
reports
• Implementation of the Biometric system has been encountering
network challenges due to lower bandwidth.
• Significant budget cuts that has led to inability to carry out some
planned activities for the 2025/26 financial year.
• Delays by MDAs in submitting key reports required for critical assessment
of revenue performance.

ROADS FUND ADMINISTRATION
Vote Number: 274
Controlling Officer: Chief Executive Officer
I. MISSION
To economically, efficiently, effectively mobilise, manage and account for
funds for the construction, maintenance and rehabilitation of public roads
in Malawi.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To ensure value for money by improving quality of works done for road
construction, maintenance and rehabilitation and improved monitoring
and evaluation systems;
• To improve transport sector financing, planning and coordination;
• To sustain good corporate governance;
• Enhance risk management and internal controls;
• To increase revenue for road rehabilitation;
• To improve RFA’s corporate image – awareness of RFA brand and
functions of the RFA; and
• To strengthen RFA Systems and procedures.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2023/24
• Completed the capacity improvement of Dr. Saulosi Klausi Chilima
Highway and Mzimba Street.
• Completed the construction of multi-carriageway Lilongwe bridge.
• Completed Feasibility and Detailed engineering design for Lilongwe -
Mchinji Road, Blantyre-Lunzu Road, and Zalewa-Mwanza Road.
• Registered tremendous progress on the following projects: Kaphatenga -
Benga section of the M005 Road, rehabilitation of the M001 road, Phase II
of the Ntcheu -Tsangano-Neno Road, Lirangwe -Chipini road, Phase I of
Rumphi-Nyika-Chitipa road and Chimwanza-Nambuma road.
• Negotiated with the Abu Dhabi Fund for Development on the upgrading
of Mangochi-Makanjira road Project.
• Increased revenue for road maintenance by 83%;
IV. PROGRAM ISSUES
• Accumulation of unpaid fuel levy affected road maintenance activities.
• Delayed payments that may lead to creation of arrears;
• Delay in relocation of services from the road reserve Encroachment of
road reserves country wide had led to vast sums of money for
compensations, and hazards to road users (accidents);
• Vandalism of culverts and other road infrastructure overstretching limited
budget;
• Unreasonable demands by utilities companies for compensation for
relocation services for new projects

=== Page 300 (pypdf) ===
300
• Delayed commencement of new road projects due to multiple approvals
of procurement.
• Fulfilling the 30% sub contracting requirement as required by NCIC has
been a challenge as most of the local subcontractors have capacity
(financial, equipment & personnel) challenges,
• Significant realignment of Local currency and fuel price increase has
resulted in increase in projects’ construction costs
• Shortage of foreign currency has affected the importation of essential
construction materials was affected by shortage of foreign currency; e.g
bitumen, steel; and
• Scarcity of fuel has affected progress and timely completion of projects
and related costs.

NATIONAL STATISTICAL OFFICE
Vote number: 276
Controlling Officer: Commissioner of Statistics
I. MISSION
To provide high quality and timely statistical information through carrying
out of censuses and surveys, setting statistical standards and to promote
dissemination and use of statistics for policy formulation, decision-making,
research transparency, and general public awareness.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To ensure continuous provision of coordinated, timely and high quality
statistics to satisfy user needs;
• To coordinate production of statistics;
• To enhance public confidence in statistics; and
• To enhance user-focused service delivery.

NATIONAL PLANNING COMMISION

Controlling Officer: Director General
I. MISSION
To formulate the medium to long term national development strategies
including ensuring effective implementation and review of these development
strategies
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To identify Malawi’s socio -economic development priorities and
formulate the national vision and strategy for social and economic goals

advantage.

strategies and the medium-term development plans.

programmes for implementation in line with the national vision and
strategies for the country; and

FINANCIAL INTELLIGENCE AUTHORITY
Vote number: 279
Controlling Officer: Director General
I. MISSION
To prevent/combat financial crimes through, detecting and investigating
these crimes, and disseminating financial intelligence to Law Enforcement
Agencies in order to maintain the integrity of the financial sector.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To enhance the generation and utilization of financial intelligence
information in order to combat financial crimes in Malawi;
• To promote a safe and sound financial system to prevent or detect
money laundering and terrorist financing activities;
• To enhance the tracing, identification, tracking, freezing, and
confiscation of proceeds of crime;
• To enhance organizational partnerships with regional and international
FIAs; and
• To enhance internal processes of FIA to improve its performance and
delivery of its functions.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2024/25
• Sixty six (66) Money Laundering (ML) cases are being investigated by Law
Enforcement Agencies (LEAs) resulting from FIA’s disseminations,
disclosures, and requests from LEAs. These arise from 32 disseminations of
financial intelligence made by the FIA to various Law Enforcement
Agencies (LEAs) and 47 requests from LEAs on cases they are pursuing
which are linked to 356 individuals.
• Nine (9) ML cases are being prosecuted by Law Enforcement Agencies
resulting from FIA’s disseminations, disclosures, and requests by LEAs. The
cases were on tax evasion, illegal externalisation of foreign exchange,
official corruption, fraud and abuse of office.
• Investigated 13 cases of which 3 came from the general public. The
cases were on financial crimes such as fraud, theft by public servant,
and money laundering, robbery and illegal externalisation of foreign
currency. These investigations led to tracing of assets (4 houses and 2
motor vehicles) linked to one case of fraud and money laundering. In
addition, the investigations led to preservation of K1billion and freezing
of 11 bank account with money amounting to K31.6million.
• Promoted public awareness of the FIA’s mandate, confiscation of assets
linked to financial crimes, and FIA’s coordination with LEAs. The FIA also
participated in TV and Radio programme organised by organised by
Centre for Continued Journalism.
• Conducted stakeholder engagement programs with various
stakeholders (Malawi Police Service, UNIMA law enforcement students,

=== Page 320 (pypdf) ===
320
and other Government Agencies. This was aimed at promoting
awareness on laws on AML/CFT and seeking implementation of the
same in various ways.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Inadequate funding to match the demand for FIA services and payment
of Malawi’s subscription to the Eastern and Southern Africa Anti -Money
Laundering Group (ESAAMLG).
• Lack of office space to accommodate the need for an increase in
staffing.
• High vacancy rate resulting into increased workload for the available
staff.
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

MINISTRY OF HEALTH AND SANITATION
Vote number: 310
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Health and Sanitation
I. MISSION
To provide strategic leadership for the delivery of a comprehensive range
of quality, equitable and efficient health services to all people in Malawi
by creating an enabling environment for health promoting activities.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To increase equitable access to and improve the quality of health services;
• To reduce environmental and social risk factors that have a direct impact
on health;
• To improve the availability and quality of health infrastructure and medical
equipment;
• To improve availability, retention, performance and motivation of human
resources for health for effective, efficient and equitable health service
delivery;
• To improve the availability, quality, and utilization of medicines and
medical supplies;
• To generate quality information and make it accessible to all intended
users for evidence -base decision -making, through standardized and
harmonized tools across all programs;
• To improve leadership and governance across the health sector and at all
levels of the healthcare system; and
• To increase health sector financial resources and improve efficiency in
resource allocation and utilization.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Implemented the Integrated Health Service Delivery Reform under HSSP
III, shifting from vertical programs to integrated, people -centred
platforms of care.
• Piloted three integrated care pathways (integrated screening, chronic
care integration, and integrated MCH clinics) in 14 facilities across
Rumphi and Nkhotakota districts.
• Increased national OPD attendance by 14%, from 22.9 million (2024) to
26.1 million (2025).
• Improved OPD visits per capita to 1.2 –1.3, reflecting enhanced access
and positive health-seeking behaviour.
• Maintained stable inpatient mortality rates at approximately 21.4 deaths
per 1,000 admissions, despite increased service utilization.
• Strengthened service availability particularly in urban districts.
• Completed construction and commissioned a Cobalt -60 radiotherapy
machine at the National Cancer Centre.
• Completed Phase I of Domasi Community Hospital, pending electricity
connection.

=== Page 330 (pypdf) ===
330
• Constructed key specialized facilities, including: Infectious Disease Unit at
QECH and Paediatric Eye Clinic at Mzuzu Central Hospital
• Completed construction of Khwisa Health Centre (Balaka) and Chiponde
Health Centre (Lilongwe), ready for handover.
• Rehabilitated 3 hospitals, 3 laboratories, and 6 staff houses in Mwanza,
Mzimba, and Mponela (Dowa).
• Procured and installed, Laundry and sterilization machines in 12 districts ,
Medical furniture for 70 health facilities , Oxygen PSA plants at Zomba
Central Hospital and Karonga District Hospital , Waste management
equipment at Nsanje District Hospital and CT Scanner at Kamuzu Central
Hospital.
• Recruited 5,992 health workers across various cadres:
• Promoted 898 health workers across different cadres.
• Supported long-term training of 131 health workers at undergraduate and
postgraduate levels.
• Rolled out the Performance Management System (PMS) across districts,
central hospitals, and headquarters.
• Developed and deployed a PMS software, integrated with the upgraded
iHRIS for automated performance tracking.
• Implemented measures to mitigate the impact of significant donor
funding cuts, particularly from the US and UK.
• Increased domestic financing allocations,
• Rolled out option-paying services to all central hospitals and 13 districts.
• Scaled up Direct Facility Financing (DFF) to all districts, mobilizing: MWK 3.3
billion from development partners and MWK 500 million from Government
resources
• Improved national medicine availability from 66% to approximately 70%
by the last quarter of FY 2025/26.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
1. Heavy reliance on external financing disrupted by donor aid cuts
2. Inadequate staffing at primary care level affecting integrated service
delivery
3. High referral burden due to incomplete district hospital infrastructure
4. Intermittent medicine stock -outs due to funding and CMST liquidity
challenges
5. Limited critical care capacity contributing to high mortality at central
hospitals
6. Rising operational costs (fuel, utilities, medical supplies) affecting facility
functionality
7. Emergency outbreaks diverting resources from planned programmes
