MINISTRY OF GENDER, CHILDREN, DISABILITY AND SOCIAL WELFARE
Vote Number: 320
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Gender, Children, Disability and Social
Welfare
I. MISSION
To accelerate inclusive socioeconomic development for the people of
Malawi through promotion of gender equality, child and social protection
using community and welfare approaches.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To increase meaningful participation of all gender groups in decision
making, wealth creation and poverty reduction;
• To increase community participation in development programs
• To improve capacity of households to manage their livelihoods, nutrition
wellbeing and HIV;
• To improve access to social justice and welfare services;
• To improve wellbeing of the vulnerable and disadvantaged groups;
• To improve equitable access to quality child development and
protection services;
• To improve coordination of policy and program development and
review; and
• To improve the quality of life of persons with disabilities and older persons.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Launched the Ministry’s Strategic Plan (2024-2030);
• Launched the National Gender Policy (2025-2030);
• Launched the National Integrated Early Childhood Development Policy
(2025-2030);
• Launched the National Actional Plan on Women’s Economic
Empowerment (2025-2030);
• Disbursed grants and procured items to successful Disability Trust Fund
(DTF) applicants across the country for the second-round cycle;
• Trained 38 visually impaired individuals in vocational skills at the Mulanje
Vocational and Rehabilitation Centre for the Blind;
• Launched the National Action Plan on Inclusion and Protection of the
Elderly and the Older Persons Act; and
• Conducted strategic advocacy workshops for 151 traditional and
religious leaders (86 men, 65 women) on ending child marriage.

=== Page 344 (pypdf) ===
344
IV. PROGRAM ISSUES
• Continued delays in the processing of payments in the new payment
system (IFMIS) hence affecting implementation of the Ministry’s
programmes and effective functioning of all the institutions under the
Ministry;
• Mobility challenges due to inadequate number of utility vehicles across
departments and increased cost of maintenance; and
• Lack of enumeration for Early Childhood Development caregivers where
only 2000 out of 49,000 caregivers receive honorarium; and
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

MINISTRY OF INFORMATION AND COMMUNICATIONS TECHNOLOGY
Vote number: 330
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Information and Communications Technology
I. MISSION
To promote and facilitate access to public information, spearhead the
development of ICT products and services through utilization of innovative
information and digital communication technologies for sustainable socio -
economic development.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To provide an enabling environment for information, communication
and technology development;
• To improve access to correct, relevant and timely public information for
popular participation in the development processes;
• To promote access and use of E -Services, ICT infrastructure and
governance; and
• To improve institutional capacity and organizational efficiency of the
Ministry.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26 FY
• Developed 7 systems as follows:
o DPMIS System for Ministry of Foreign Affairs;
o Attendance and Travel Booking Modules in eGov360
platform;
o Client-Centricity System;
o E-Pay Slip System;
o Mobile App for AIP;
o Active Directory Management system,
o Inventory system for DMAP
• Developed 6 websites namely:
o OPC Main Website;
o Open Government Partnership (OGP),
o Website for OPC,
o NLGFC website,
o Local Government website,
o Civil service Commission Website
• Fibre maintenance undertaken in all affected sites including Capital
hill
• Initiated the development and review of key legal and regulatory
frameworks, namely: Ministry Strategic Plan, National Information
and Communication policy and Strategy, National Postal Policy,

National Child -Online Protection Strategy, National Broadband
Strategy, National Digital Broadcasting Policy; the National
Cybersecurity Policy, and Strategy;

mandate of the Public Relation Officers (PROs).
• Constructed 35 telecommunication towers out of a target of 136 across
all regions in the country.

MINISTRY OF HOMELAND SECURITY
Vote number: 340
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Homeland Security
I. MISSION
To provide safety and security for all persons through the provision of
internationally accepted identification system; protection of life and
property; safe and humane custody of offenders, regulation of entry, exit
and movement of persons and management of refugees.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To improve infrastructure in security institutions (Police, Immigration and
Prisons);
• To formulate legislative framework and policies to ensure national safety
and security;
• promote safety and security;
• To improve correctional services;
• o regulate entry, exit and movement of persons in Malawi;
• To provide an internationally acceptable identification system; and
• To enhance management of refugees and asylum seekers.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/2026
• Reviewed the Trafficking in Persons shelter regulations;
• Trained law enforcement officers;
• Inspected Trafficking in Persons shelters across the country to ensure
compliance with minimum protection standards, in preparation for
gazettement;
• Inspected Mwanza, Ntcheu, Dedza, Nsanje, Bvumbwe, Thyolo,
Makande, Chikwawa, Zomba Central Prison, Mikuyu I, Mikuyu II,
Mpyupyu, Blantyre and Domasi prisons.;
• Held successfully bilateral meeting with Mozambique on Joint
Permanent Commission on Defense and Security (JPCDS) . These
meetings provide a platform to address common security challenges,
promote cross -border cooperation and enhance regional stability
where key issues such as border management, combating transnational
organized crime, Trafficking in Persons and mutua l defense strategies
were agreed;
• Rehabilitated the water piping network at Area 30 Police Headquarters
in readiness for the installation of prepaid meters;
• Acquired 290 hectares of land from Senior Chief Mwenewenya in Kayilizi
– Chitipa for the establishment of a new Refugee Camp;
• Conducted five (5) audits out of the six (6) audits planned which
revealed reduced audit queries as most of the internal control
deficiencies were already addressed.

=== Page 370 (pypdf) ===
370
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
▪ Delays in the processing of payments by the Accountant General’s
Office, which negatively affected the implementation of activities;
▪ Inadequate funding for the relocation of refugees and asylum seekers
from Dzaleka Camp in Dowa to Kailizi in Chitipa. In addition, the
construction of the new Blantyre Police Station and Chitedze Maximum
Prison was underfunded, leading to delays in project implementation;
▪ Inadequate office space, resulting in high operational costs.
▪ Shortage of operational vehicles for coordination, monitoring, and
evaluation activities.
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

MALAWI POLICE SERVICE
Vote number: 341
Controlling Officer: Inspector General of Police
I. MISSION
• To provide quality policing services in partnership with the community
and stakeholders.
II. STRATEGIC GOALS
• To enhance Public Safety and Security Services.
• To enhance Management and Administration Services.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• MPS recorded 3.3% decrease of crime, from 43,629 cases in 2024 to 42,170
criminal cases in 2025.
• Reduced crime rate by 12, from 215 per 100,000 in 2024 to 203 per 100,000 in
2025, leading to reduced fear of crime among citizens.
• MPS recorded 6% decrease of road traffic accidents, from 4,246 accidents
in 2024 to 3,984 accidents in 2025.
• Recorded decrease of number of deaths due to road traffic accidents from
1,055 to 1,033.
• Strengthened community policing structures, supported the establishment of
traffic wardens, enhanced rural police units, and improved child protection
and victim support services.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Inadequate Funding to meet operational demands is critically
hampering service delivery and performance.
• High inflation rates have eroded the real value of the MPS budget by
approximately 50%, drastically reducing purchasing power and the
Service's ability to procure essential equipment, materials, and services
necessary for effective policing.
• Critical shortage of motor vehicles has created significant mobility
challenges across the Service. This has severely compromised the MPS's
capacity to respond promptly to emergencies.

MALAWI PRISONS SERVICE
Vote number: 342
Controlling Officer: Commissioner General of Prisons
I. MISSION
To contribute to public security and safety through effective
rehabilitation, reformation, development and community
reintegration of offenders.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To enhance public security through safe and humane custody of
offenders;
• To create conducive environment for enhanced offender rehabilitation
services in prisons;
• To expand and improve offender reformation programmes;
• To strengthen institutional socioeconomic and infrastructure
development;
• To establish and roll out effective community reintegration of offenders;
and
• To mainstream crosscutting issues of HIV/AIDS, gender and climate
change.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Continued offering rehabilitation and reintegration services to 23% of
convicted prisoner population to increase prisoners’ opportunity for
employment and address reoffending.
• Continued with the provision of health services to inmates on
coordinated efforts with District Health Offices (DHO) including
undertaking of screening exercises for Cholera, HIV/AIDS, Tuberculosis
and sexually transmitted diseases in compliance to the Uni ted Nations
Standard Minimum Rules for the treatment of Prisoners.
• Improved prisoners welfare through the rehabilitation of Kasungu Prison;
and
• Successfully hoisted the Joint Permanent Commission on Defence and
Security between Malawi and Zambia
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Overcrowding of prisons currently at 100% compromised prison health
and affected effective implementation of rehabilitation and
reintegration programs;
• Acute shortage of institutional houses and inadequate operational
vehicles affected prison security and offender access to justice;

=== Page 390 (pypdf) ===
390
• General increase of costs of goods and services affected provision of
basic amenities such as food, uniforms, blankets, cups and plates to
inmates; and
• High vacancies in the service affected rolling out of community
outreach programmes.
V. PROGRAM STRUCTURE

DEPARTMENT OF IMMIGRATION
Vote number: 343
Controlling Officer: Director General
I. MISSION
• To provide effective and efficient migration, citizenship, boarder control
management and issuance of travel documents in accordance with the
laws of Malawi and international best practices.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To improve systems, operations and capacity of the organization to
meet international standards.
• To establish effective policies and systems for securing safe movement
of people across borders, ensuring compliance with legal frameworks,
and promoting humane treatment of migrants.
• To enhance client satisfaction and engagement through personalized
and accessible services.
• To enhance innovation and organizational growth through targeted
research, strategic planning and development initiatives.
• To streamline and optimize border control processes that enhance
security and lawful cross border movements.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Procured a marine speed boat.
• Recruited 356 officers.
• Rolled out e-passport.
• Launched new Migration Information and Data Analysis System (MIDAS)
system to boost proper migration processes and prevent irregular and
irregular migration in Malawi
• Offices for Immigration have been secured in Kasungu, Nkhotakota,
Phalombe and Thyolo.
• Conducted awareness campaigns.
• Established border posts in strategic areas such as Namizana, Likangala
and Katuli.
• Reduced cases of human trafficking
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES-
• Inadequate vehicles.
• Inadequate housing for officers.
• Inadequate infrastructure. The Department does not have its own office
buildings across all regions of the country and therefore relies on rented
premises.
• Influx of irregular migrants.

=== Page 400 (pypdf) ===
400
• The Department continues to use an archaic legislation (Immigration Act
1964 and Citizenship Act -1966). This creates some gaps on migration
between developments and the law.
• Legislative gap. There is currently no Passport Act in place to regulate
the processing and issuance of travel documents.
• There are delays in approvals for permits.
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

NATIONAL REGISTRATION BUREAU
Vote number: 344
Controlling Officer: Principal Secretary for National Registration Bureau
I. MISSION
To ensure all Malawians and resident foreigners have access to legal
identity through efficient, accessible and secure national registration
and identification services for promotion of good governance and
sustainable socio-economic development.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To improve civil registration to achieve universal coverage and
inclusivity.
• To improve identity registration to achieve universal and inclusive legal
Identity.
• To strengthen legislative and policy frameworks for inclusive and
accessible national registration and identification services.
• To advance secure, interoperable and scalable foundational system
that drives robust, inclusive and transformative digital public
infrastructure.
• To improve communication, education and civic awareness for
increased uptake of registration services.
• To enhance the institutional capacity of the NRB for improved service
delivery.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENT IN 2025/26 FY
• Streamed various subsidy programs and social protection programs and
national IDs such as Farm Input Subsidy Program and Social Cash
Transfer.
• Registered 349,496 citizens and replaced 119,597national IDs.
• Printed and dispatched 606,630 IDs for distribution by the District
Registration Offices.
• Registered 703 resident foreigners and printed 948 foreigner ID cards.
• Registered 36,793 deaths and printed 9,814 death certificates.
• Registered 565,586 births and printed 220,449 birth certificates.
• Integrated the National Registration and Identification System with the
following 8 public and 17 private functional system: E -Government
(Enterprise Service Bus), Immigration Department, Malawi Revenue
Authority (MRA), Ministry of Agriculture (AIP), Natio nal Economic
Empowerment Fund (NEEF), Ministry of Lands, Ministry of Trade (MELTS),
Department of Registrar General, Standard Bank, First Capital Bank, FDH
Bank, Centenary Bank Malawi, National Bank of Malawi, Ecobank, NBS

=== Page 408 (pypdf) ===
408
Bank, Old Mutual, Givedirectly, Nagowa Finance Limited, Mobile Trading
System integration, Credit Reference Bureau, Laboratory management
Information System, Airtel Malawi, TNM, MCCCI and Mukuru.
• Produced and distributed I.E.C materials including Posters, T/Shirts, Golf
Shirts, Reflector Vests, Rollup banners and Fisher Jacket to support NRB
routine and outreach activities.
• Engaged National Publications Limited and Blantyre Newspaper Limited
to publish news articles aimed at promoting a positive image of NRB and
highlighting its success stories.
• Conducted loud hailing activities and market campaigns across the
country.
• Implemented SMS alerts through TNM and Airtel on registration and
collection of National IDs.
• Developed and launched the 2025- 2030 NRB’s Strategic Plan.
• Facilitated the amended of the Marriage, Divorce and Family Relations
Act to allow registration of marriages by the National Registration
Bureau.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Inadequate office space for both Headquarters and District
Registration Offices. A project proposal to renovate DROs has been
submitted to PSIP for their consideration;
• Absence of field offices for NRB rendering its services inaccessible to the
communities. To address this NRB is piloting the establishment of offices
at Traditional Authority level;
• Limited printing capacity of IDs due to frequent breakdown of printers
leading to delays in printing and issuance of IDs; NRB is therefore
planning to procure 3 high end ID printers with support from partners;
• Low registration of births and deaths because of limited human
resources/understaffing. To address this, NRB has gone through a
functional review that will enable expansion of its services through
expanded staffing; and
• Unreliable internet connectivity leading to NRB registration centres
inability to send registration data to NRB HQ in real time.
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

MINISTRY OF JUSTICE AND CONSTITUTIONAL AFFAIRS
Vote number: 350
Controlling Officer: Solicitor General and Secretary for Justice
I. MISSION
To promote the rule of law through the provision of professional legal
services to Government and other stakeholders in a transparent and
accountable manner in order to ensure a just, fair and democratic society.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To provide sound legal advice to the Government;
• To prepare legislation and draft legal instruments on behalf of
Government;
• To provide political, policy and technical oversight to the Democratic
Governance Sector; and
• To enhance and strengthen services through the provision of policy
guidance and administrative support.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26.
• Drafted and published 14 Bills for enactment by Parliament.
• Processed publication of 25 Acts of Parliament following their
enactment, including Mental Health Act, Prisons Act, Presidential,
Parliamentary and Local Government (Amendment) Act, Sugarcane
Industry Act, Construction Industry Act, Foreign Exchange Act an d
Tourism Act.
• Drafted and published 98 pieces of subsidiary legislation, including
Regulations, Directives, Orders and Notices.
• Saved Government funds amounting to MK111.6 billion from
concluded civil litigation cases.
• Submitted three State Party Reports to international treaty bodies - The
Committee on the Rights of the Child, The Human Rights Council under
the 4th Universal Periodic Review process, and The Committee on the
Protection of All Persons from Enforced Disappearance
• Successfully underwent the 4th cycle Universal Periodic Review before
the Human Rights Council in November, 2025.
• Produced and disseminated the 2025 Justice and Accountability
Survey Report.
• Prosecuted over 1,825 criminal cases (626 homicides, 999 other
applications and 200 other offences).
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Inadequate human resource, including lawyers and officers in other
departments (Internal Audit, procurement, accounts, administration,
etc).
• Inadequate reliable vehicles to facilitate delivery of the mandate of the
Ministry, including court attendance by lawyers.

=== Page 416 (pypdf) ===
416
• Low lawyer retention rate due to non-competitive conditions of service.
This challenge is compounded by lack of uniformity of conditions of
service within the Public Service resulting in most of the lawyers being lost
to other public institutions.
• Lack of conducive working environment caused by critical shortage of
office space, this has resulted in up to 4 officers sharing an office.
• Inadequate budget allocation to support effective and efficient
delivery of the mandate of the Ministry, this challenge has, among other
things, negatively impacted on the plan to open additional regional
and district offices.

DIRECTORATE OF PUBLIC PROSECUTIONS AND STATE ADVOCATE
Vote number: 351
Controlling Officer: Solicitor General and Secretary for Justice
I. MISSION
To contribute to socio - economic development and the achievement of
justice, peace and security in the country through timely and expeditious
prosecution of criminal cases and provision of advisory legal services to the
Government.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To prosecute persons accused of crime generally and those accused of
committing homicide offences and financial crimes specifically;
• To provide legal and technical advice as well as relevant consents to
the Malawi Police Service, Malawi Revenue Authority, Anti -Corruption
Bureau, Wildlife and National Parks, and other law enforcement
agencies;
• To provide legal advice to Ministries and Departments in Criminal
matters;
• To augment Malawi’s standing as a Regional Partner in the fight against
crime generally and transnational crime in particular through enhanced
participation in international legal cooperation and mutual legal
assistance endeavours;
• To enhance access to timely justice for victims of crime through efficient
and timely granting of consents to prosecute to private Legal
Practitioners;
• To facilitate deterrence in crime through Public Awareness; and
• To enhance the use of asset forfeiture laws.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Prosecuted 996 cases as of January, 2025 against an annual target of
1,300;
• Re-established good working environment among law enforcement
agencies;
• Attended to over 91% of appeals, bail application, and confirmation of
cases;
• Provided legal and technical advice to requisite Law Enforcement
Agencies (LEAs) and granted relevant consents to prosecute for the
Malawi Police Service and Department of Parks and Wildlife; and
• Trained 40 public prosecutors/state advocates on Case Plus
Management Systems for tracking cases.

=== Page 424 (pypdf) ===
424
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Poor working conditions for lawyers as compared to private practice
remunerations therefore making retention difficult;
• Insufficient equipment and ICT infrastructure for a comprehensive Case
Management System;
• The lack of measurement criteria for ‘load’ of financial crimes cases,
which might result in lack of appreciation of the efficiency and
effectiveness of the Directorate hence affecting morale of professional
staff; and
• Mobility challenges due to insufficient number and poor condition of
vehicles.
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

DEPARTMENT OF REGISTRAR GENERAL
Vote number: 352
Controlling Officer: Registrar General
I. MISSION
To provide a well-regulated and viable environment for doing business and an
excellent and friendly service to the public in the registration of industrial
property rights, commercial and not for profit entities, civil events and the
collection of non-tax revenue.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To improve registration, storage, retrieval, management and dissemination
of information through computerised databases and systems (i.e.) industrial
property, business registration and civil registration;
• To promote the awareness and utilization of, as well as respect for Industrial
Property Rights in order to stimulate technological innovation and
development and enhance Malawian industry and commerce through the
implementation of National IP Policy;
• To promote and supporting the harmonization of domestic and
international law, procedure and practice in order to enhance
participation in global trade markets and attract foreign direct investment
and technology inflows into the country.

ADMINISTRATOR GENERAL’S DEPARTMENT
Vote number: 353
Controlling Officer: The Administrator General
I. MISSION
To efficiently, equitably and transparently administer deceased estates
and other trusts in accordance with the law.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To improve service delivery and beneficiary access to deceased estates
including, among other strategies, automation of the deceased estates
management system;
• To explore forms of dispute settlement compatible to disputes in
deceased estates;
• To increase knowledge levels on deceased estate management and
distribution amongst the citizenry; and
• To strengthen institutional capacity.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS 2025/26
• Paid out and closed a total of 1,800 files against the annual target of
1500;
• received a total of MK20 billion for various deceased estates against the
annual target of 18 billion;
• Paid out a total sum of MK17 billion against the annual target of 16 billion;
• Additional 120 minor’s accounts were opened and a total of 150 minor’s
accounts were closed.
• 200 disputes related to deceased estates were registered against the
annual target of 150 and 107 were resolved and concluded against the
annual target of 120.
• Collected the sum of MK12 million as revenue against the annual target
of MK20 million.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• None availability of ICT staff is affecting the implementation of the
automated system;
• Delayed and reduced funding and processing of payments by treasury;
• Vacancies in key posts is negatively affecting effective service delivery;
and
• Mobility challenges to fully deliver on the Department’s mandate;
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE
Table 5.1: Budget by Programme and Sub-Programme (MK 000‘000)

ATTORNEY GENERAL’S DEPARTMENT
Vote number: 354
Controlling Officer: Solicitor General and Secretary for Justice
I. MISSION
To contribute to socio -economic development and achieve justice,
peace and security in the country in collaboration with justice stakeholders.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To provide sound legal advice to the Government;
• To provide Civil Litigation Services timely; and
• To provide political, policy and technical oversight to the Democratic
Governance Sector.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS 2025/26
• Saved Government funds amounting to MK 111.6 billion from
concluded civil litigation cases, and
• Cleared 44 old/outstanding civil cases.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Inadequate human resource, including lawyers.
• Inadequate reliable vehicles to facilitate delivery of the mandate of the
Department, including court attendance by lawyers.
• Low lawyer retention rate due to non-competitive conditions of service.
This challenge is compounded by lack of uniformity of conditions of
service within the Public Service resulting in most of the lawyers being lost
to other public institutions.
• Lack of conducive working environment caused by critical shortage of
office space, this has resulted in up to 4 officers sharing an office.
• Inadequate budget allocation to support effective and efficient
delivery of the mandate of the Department, this challenge has, among
other things, negatively impacted on the plan to open additional
regional and district offices.

OFFICE OF THE REGISTRAR OF POLITICAL PARTIES
Vote number: 355
Controlling Officer: Registrar of Political Parties
I. MISSION
To promote the role of political parties as critical players in the development
of Malawi through registration, regulation and capacity development of
political parties.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To register and keep documentation of political parties.
• To regulate political parties
• To conduct civic education on the Political Parties Act and its
Regulations
• To develop the institutional capacity of political parties
• To enhance and strengthen services of the ORPP as a pivotal office in
democratic governance through the provision of policy guidance and
administrative support.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Recruited 7 officers on secondment and commenced recruitment of 16
permanent staff.
• Developed and gazetted Political Parties (Complaints Handling)
regulations
• Registered 1 Political Party.
• Conducted Political Parties Act sensitization campaigns in 21 districts
nationwide.
• Prepared and submitted three (3) prosecutable case files to the Director
of Public Prosecutions.
• Participated in continental efforts to establish an association of political
party regulatory bodies with Kenya and Sierra Leone to support peer
learning and capacity building.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Late disbursements of funds
• Delayed payments to service providers
• Lack of vehicles / transport related challenges
• Shortage of specialized human resource appropriate to the mandate of
the office
• Inadequate funding for operations of the office i.e. for development of
regulations, consultations, civic education and investigations of
complaints

COMPANIES REGISTRATION AND INTELLECTUAL PROPERTY CENTRE
Vote number: 356
Controlling Officer: Registrar General
I. MISSION
To provide a well -regulated and viable environment for doing business
and an excellent and friendly service to the public in the registration of
industrial property rights, commercial and not for profit entities, civil events
and the collection of non-tax revenue.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To improve registration, storage, retrieval, management and
dissemination of information through computerised databases and
systems (i.e.) industrial property, business registration and civil
registration;
• To promote the awareness and utilization of, as well as respect for
Industrial Property Rights in order to stimulate technological innovation
and development and enhance Malawian industry and commerce
through the implementation of National IP Policy;
• To promote and supporting the harmonization of domestic and
international law, procedure and practice in order to enhance
participation in global trade markets and attract foreign direct
investment and technology inflows into the country.

MINISTRY OF TOURISM
Vote number: 360
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Tourism
I. MISSION
To develop and promote tourism, conserve and manage wildlife so as to
ultimately turn Malawi into an attractive and competitive tourism
destination in the region.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To develop and promote the tourism industry in a sustainable manner for
increased contribution of tourism to economic growth and foreign
exchange earnings;
• To conserve, manage, utilise and raise awareness of wildlife and its values
in and out;
• Side of National Parks, Wildlife Reserves and Nature Sanctuaries through
sound ecological management; and
• To strengthen institutional capacity
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS FOR 2025/26
• Collaborative efforts with the Ministry of Lands and local councils, have
resulted in the acquisition and zoning of 52.7 hectares of land for tourism
development. This is in line with the Tourism Master Plan, which aims to
strategically develop tourism sites across the country.
• A co-management arrangement for Nyika and Vwaza National Parks
has been finalized under the Public Private Partnership Framework (PPF).
• To mitigate human -wildlife conflict, a 90 km perimeter fence has been
successfully completed in Vwaza Marsh Wildlife Reserve. Additionally,
106 km of a 130 km fence has been constructed in Kasungu National
Park.
• A contract has been signed for the development of an eco -friendly
picnic site at Domwe Island, with a budget of $60,000.
• Revenue collection in protected areas has been migrated to electronic
payment systems and Foreign Currency Denominated Accounts
(FCDAs) have been opened to automate payments for tourism
enterprises dealing with foreign clients,
• An Electronic Monitoring System for the gaming industry is under
development, with modules already created. This system will enhance
oversight and ensure compliance within the gaming sector.
• Enhanced Destination Campaigns focusing on the regional and
international Markets.
• Undertook a comprehensive redevelopment of Malawi tourisms official
website www.visitmalawi.mw
• Provided sector specific Incentives that deliver preferential rates and
packages that stimulate the growth of domestic tourism

=== Page 464 (pypdf) ===
464
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Securing land and providing basic support infrastructure for the 103
project sites identified in the National Tourism Investment Master Plan
remains a challenge due to limited financial and technical resources.
This has slowed progress in making these sites investor-ready.
• Delays in funding and the processing of funds have derailed the
implementation of Public Sector Investment Programme (PSIP) projects,
including critical infrastructure developments such as access roads and
tourism facilities.
• There is a lack of incentives to promote niche tourism segments, such as
sport tourism, which could diversify the tourism offering and attract new
markets.
• Poor infrastructure, such as inadequate access roads, has resulted in low
tourist numbers to protected areas, limiting revenue generation and
conservation efforts.
• Human-wildlife conflict remains a significant challenge, leading to
animosity between communities and wildlife officers. While perimeter
fencing has been completed in some areas, more efforts are needed to
address this issue comprehensively.
• Many tourism operators are not complying with levy remittance
requirements, leading to revenue losses for the sector.
• The lack of robust systems to track payments, due to a centralized and
outdated system, has hindered effective revenue collection and
management.
• Climate change and environmental degradation pose significant
threats to tourism assets, particularly in protected areas and along Lake
Malawi.

MINISTRY OF LABOUR, SKILLS AND INNOVATION
Vote number: 370
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Labour, Skills and Innovation
I. MISSION
To develop and support harmonious relations and skilled human resources
through enhancement of social justice, peace, decent work, technical and
vocational training to ensure high quality, healthy and productive labour
force.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To coordinate employment creation; To enhance compliance to labour
standards;
• To enhance child labour elimination;
• To improve quality of lathe enrollment in Community Skills Development
Centers stands at 916; 5969 in Community Technical Colleges and 5797 in
National Technical Colleges.
• To improve Occupational Safety, Health and Welfare (OSHW);
• To improve workers’ compensation; and
• To improve efficiency and effectiveness of service delivery.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Managed to send 820 youths to work in Israeli farms;
• Sent 345 youths to Qatar, 391 to UAE and 94 to Kuwait for work;
• 4,627 fresh graduates have undergone graduate internship;
• Operationalized Ngabu Community Technical College in Chikwawa;
• Expanded the annual formal enrolment in technical colleges from 3,809 in
2020 to 10,400 in 2025;
• Constructed four Centres of Vocational Excellence (CoVEs) through the EU-
funded Zantchito Project.
• A total of 5,732 young people were trained using the out of school informal
training in 2025 and Government targets to train over 7000 young people in
2026;
• Conducted timely administration of July and November,2025 TEVET
examination series.
• Issued a total of 20000 Malawi TEVET certificates to successful candidates
thereby clearing the certification backlog by 90%.
• Facilitated the return of all 60 women who were stuck in other countries due
to unregulated work procedures;
• Removed 309 children from child labor; and
• Registered 638 workplaces and collected MK 146,055,100,000.00 in revenues
for the registration and inspection of pressure vessels.

• Absence of vehicles for conducting labour inspections;
• Poor and inadequate infrastructure and equipment for TEVET; and
• Inadequate teaching and support staff in the TEVET institutions.

MINISTRY OF INDUSTRIALISATION, BUSINESS, TRADE AND TOURISM
Vote number: 390
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Industrialization, Business, Trade and Tourism
I. MISSION
To promote inclusive, sustainable, and competitive industrial, trade and
tourism development that contributes to the structural transformation of
Malawi’s economy
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• Growth and diversification of exports promoted, and international
market share of Malawian products and services expanded;
• Increased contribution of exports as % of GDP from 14.6% to 20%
• Increased exports of non -agricultural products to exports from
43.2% to 65% and agriculture exports other than tobacco from 20%
to 30%
• Increased contribution of manufacturing to GDP from 11.5% to
12.6% ;
• Increased employment in manufacturing from 13.8% to 16.1%
• Increased FDI from USA 112.3m to USD500m,
• Increased domestic credit to private sector from 10.5% to 27.8%
• Improved business environment for trade and private sector
development;
• Enhanced participation of Malawians in economic activities;
• Increase MSMEs market access from 20% to 50%; and
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Finalised the Special Economic Zones Regulations
• Developed of the Automated Business Licensing System
• Finalised the Mchinji, Dedza, and Mwanza one stop border posts
• Developed of Electronic National Single Window System
• Malawi Trade Portal was upgraded and is now live.
• Rollout of the National MSME Management Information System
• Establised of six new Business Support Units
• Finalized and commissioned value addition projects in Blantyre
• Conducted a Banana Sector Mapping exercise
• Finalised the National Investment Policy
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Inadequate resources for compensation and the provision of utility
infrastructure within Special Economic Zones, as well as delays in
fully servicing land required for investor occupation.
• High cost of industrial infrastructure
• Persistent cost and reliability challenges in utilities constrain private
sector growth and investor confidence.

MINISTRY OF TRANSPORT AND PUBLIC WORKS
Vote number: 400
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Transport and Public Works
I. MISSION
To facilitate the provision of a safe, efficient, sustainable transport systems and
building structures through the formulation and oversight of policy in order to
promote socio-economic development.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To ensure an integrated, well- managed, viable and sustainable transport
infrastructure meeting national and regional goals including the
promotion of inter-modal competition or complementarities;
• To ensure the provision of safe, reliable, effective and efficient transport
operations which best meet demand and facilitate economic activity;
• To develop transport corridors in order to improve the competitiveness of
Malawian goods and services on the regional and international markets
and lower the cost of imports;
• To ensure that cross-cutting issues are mainstreamed in sectorial strategies
and activities to promote a socially and environmentally sustainable and
climate resilient transport system; and
• To ensure the provision of project management services in building
construction.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Rehabilitation and upgrading of 72km of the existing railway alignment
between Marka and Bangula station - physical Progress was at 40.01%
with 8km of track layed.
• Constructed Likoma Port advanced with overall progress of works at 90%
as at 31st December, 2024.
• Constructed passenger canopy is nearing completion at Kamuzu
International Airport.
• Completed construction of Capital Hill Clinic.
IV. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

MALAWI HUMAN RIGHTS COMMISSION
Vote number: 430
Controlling Officer: Executive Secretary
I. MISSION
To lead in the promotion and protection of human rights in an independent
and professional manner
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• Improved protection and investigations of human rights violations;
• Improved access to information and gender compliance;
• Strengthened partnerships; and
• Strengthened institutional capacity.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• The Commission reduced the backlog of human rights complaints
reported by investigating 185 cases representing 36 percent of the
Commission’s backlog.
• Conducted a situation analysis of the right to food focusing on the food
and hunger situation in the selected districts.
• Developed a human rights monitoring tool for the 2025 General
Elections.
• Conducted 10 community awareness meetings on gender and other
related laws.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Inadequate office space for both Headquarters and Regional offices;
and
• Inadequate vehicles and old fleet of vehicles.
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

MALAWI ELECTORAL COMMISSION
Vote number: 460
Controlling Officer: Chief Elections Officer
I. MISSION
To deliver credible elections according to the laws of Malawi to entrench
democratic values by implementing MEC’s activities in a free, fair,
transparent, cost-effective and inclusive manner.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To deliver justifiable, peaceful, free, fair and credible elections.
• To strengthen institutional operations and financial sustainability
through improved governance, leadership, human and financial
resources management.
• To attain pub lic trust and achieve excellent step and increased
participation of all stakeholders in management of elections and
referenda.
• To innovate and introduce digitalisation for achievement of cost
effectiveness, efficiency, transparency and security of electoral
processes and services.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS 2024/25
• Successfully registered more than 7,200,000 voters.
• Procured 6,500 election management devices.
• Procured generators.
• Enhanced capacity building of staffs through various trainings.

MINISTRY OF NATURAL RESOURCES
Vote number: 470
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Natural Resources
I. MISSION
To develop, promote and manage Natural Resources, Environment and
Climate Change for the nation through appropriate technologies,
standards and mechanisms for sustainable and equitable Socio -
economic development
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES.
• To provide an enabling framework for promoting the participation of local
communities, civil society and the private sector in forests and fisheries
conservation and management;
• To enhance compliance to the environment and natural resources
legislative and regulatory framework;
• To provide efficient and responsive weather and climate services including
provision of information on climate change; and
• Increasing annual fish production from capture fisheries and aquaculture,
reducing fish post -harvest losses and increasing per capita fish
consumption;
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Protected 88 Forest Reserves , covering a total area of 918,400 hectares,
from illegal activities through enforcement of Forest Legislation . This
included conducting 120 camping operations and 1,983 routine patrols .
Made 193 arrests, 10 vehicles impounded and confiscation of illegal items
including charcoal bags, pit saws, and timber, among others.
• Protected 90,000 hectares of forest plantations from bush fires through
maintenance of 709.5 km of firebreak and establishment of fire standby
teams.
• 4,284,958 tree seedlings produced in Government plantations in
preparation for the 2025/26 National Forestry Season
• Produced 48,510,476 tree seedlings on customary estates in readiness for
the 2025/26 National Forestry Season.
• Rehabilitated 6,790.02 hectares through natural regeneration
management along river lines, stream banks, and other degraded areas
on customary estates.
• Conducted structural and Functional review of the Forestry Department.
• Recruited and trained 21 Assistant Meteorologists (Grade K)
• Developed and disseminated the 2025/2026 National and Downscaled
Seasonal Forecast
• Opened new principal weather stations in Balaka and Neno (Nsanje and
Phalombe underway)

=== Page 514 (pypdf) ===
514
• Installed 46 new rain gauges
• Reaching 800,000 farmers with agricultural adversaries based on weather
and climate information in 16 districts on weekly basis
• Drought monitoring tool under Anticipatory Action interventions for food
security in five districts
• Developed QMS quality policy which will lead to the rolling out of QMS for
the whole department
• Finalized the DCCMS Strategic Plan for 2025-2030
• National Meteorological Policy is under review
• Meteorological Bill is under development
• Continued provision of Aviation Weather Services - over 200 flights per
month
• Malawi strengthened its participation in international carbon markets
through the development of an Article 6 Framework, which guided the
registration of 47 carbon developers and authorized six projects with
potential to generate 5.9 million carbon credits valued between USD 40–60
million. The Ministry also advanced policy and planning by initiating the
review of Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), drafting the Climate
Change Bill, and progressing on the National Adaptation Plan. Community
resilience was enhanced through initiatives such as LIFE -AR and
TRANSFORM, which supported districts with irrigation schemes, water supply
systems, beekeeping clubs, tree nurseries, fish ponds, and efficient stoves,
thereby strengthening adaptation and livelihoods. Transparency in climate
governance was improved through the launch of an online reporting
platform and preparation of key reports under the Paris Agreement.
• The Ministry rolled out the Biodiversity Finance Initiative (BIOFIN), producing
a financing plan with 11 innovative solutions, including payment for
ecosystem services, to diversify funding sources. The National Biodiversity
Strategy and Action Plan was reviewed to align with the Kunming-Montreal
Global Biodiversity Framework, while inspections of GM maize trials ensured
compliance with biosafety regulations. A study on harmful subsidies was
launched to guide reforms, and access and benefit-sharing initiatives under
the Nagoya Protocol promoted sustainable use of biological resources,
ensuring communities benefit economically and ecologically.
• Launched its National E-Waste Management Policy to address the growing
challenge of electronic waste. The country continued implementing the
Hydrochlorofluorocarbon Phase -out Plan under the Montreal Protocol,
trained customs officers and refrigeration exper ts to manage controlled
substances safely. Enforcement of the thin plastics ban was strengthened
following a Supreme Court ruling, paving the way for stricter regulation of
plastic pollution and improved environmental protection.
• Trained 47 journalists on environment and climate change reporting,
enhancing public communication and awareness.
• Several international environmental days were commemorated, including
World Ozone Day, World Environment Day, International Biodiversity Day,
and International E-Waste Day, which served as platforms for dialogue and
action. School-based initiatives and nat ional task teams promoted plastic

=== Page 515 (pypdf) ===
515
waste management, supported by inventories, training, and educational
video resources.
• 25 large scale commercial fishing units have been licensed to tap the
offshore deep-water fish resources generating MK7,570,000. In addition, 750
small scale fishing licenses and 106 sanitary certificates have been issued
generating MK58,896,000 against the annual target of MK52,000,000.
• Coordinated the production of 6,937 metric tonnes of fish by small -scale
aquaculture producers from ponds.
• Coordinated the production of 10,779,745 fingerlings that originated from
both public and private hatcheries.
• 19 enterprise groups with a total of 673 fish value chain actors (M=327; F=346
with 89 as youths) were reached with training in Cooperative Member
Education (CME) and so far, 18 groups have formally registered with the
Register of Cooperatives and have certificates.
• 9,306 farmers/fishers have been trained in various general aspects, 35,835
fish value chain actors have been reached with various awareness
campaign messages and 4,055 farmers/fishers have adopted various new
technologies ranging from fish farming to fish processing.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
● Erratic and inadequate ORT Funding
● Unfinalized legislation
● High vacancy rate
● Limited functionality of weather and climate observing systems: Automatic
Weather Stations (AWS), Lightening Detection Systems (LDS), lakebuoys
(one was washed away)
● Dilapidated office infrastructure mainly at outstations
● Failure to establish an ISO based quality management system (QMS) in
compliance with international set standards and obligations;
● Limited Mobility
● Low participation of private sector in aquaculture value chain resulting low
fish production.
● Limited accessibility to high quality farm inputs such as floating feed and
fingerlings.

MINISTRY OF MINING
Vote number: 480
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Mining
I. MISSION
To coordinate, facilitate, and promote participation of all stakeholders in
the sustainable development, utilization, and management of mineral
and petroleum resources for socioeconomic growth and development.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To ensure the sustainable management and utilization of mineral and
Petroleum resources;
• To monitor and assess geological hazards for proper planning and
instituting possible mitigation measures;
• To strengthen national, regional and international cooperation in
mineral resources management and development;
• To accelerate sustainable economic growth through mineral and
Petroleum resources exploitation; and
• To strengthen institutional capacity.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS 2025/26
• Gazetting of the Mines and Minerals Act, 2023 that lead to the
establishment of the Mines and Minerals Regulatory Authority;
• Facilitated the establishment of the Malawi Mining Investment
Company;
• The signing of Mining Development Agreement with Lotus Resources
Limited for the recommissioning of the Kayerekera Uranium Mining
Project and Lancaster Exploration Limited;
• Completion of the construction and rehabilitation of state -of-the-art
mineral processing laboratory complex;
• Formalization of Artisanal and Small -Scale Miners (ASMs) into
cooperatives;
• Exploration for Lithium occurrence in Karonga and Chitipa Area;
• Research in potential of local geological resources for ceramic ware
production. Mapping and sampling for K-feldspar/pegmatites has been
conducted in Zomba-Machinga areas;
• Mapping the extent of Graphite mineralization from Usingini to
Mbiriyawaka – Mphompha east;
• Exploration of Agro-Minerals in the Country; and
• Conducting the inaugural Malawi Mining Investment Forum (MMIF) and
Diaspora Engagement MMIF.

IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Inflation which led to cost escalation thereby affecting the planned
budget;
• Inadequate technical staff and equipment; and
• Continued illegal mining especially for gold and gemstone.

MINISTRY OF ENERGY AND MINING
Vote number: 490
Controlling Officer: Secretary for Energy and Mining
I. MISSION
Department of Energy - To coordinate, facilitate, and promote participation of all
stakeholders in the sustainable development, utilization, and management of
energy resources for socio-economic growth and development.
Department of Mining - To coordinate, facilitate, and promote participation of all
stakeholders in the sustainable development, utilization, and management of
mineral and petroleum resources for socioeconomic growth and development.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To ensure the sustainable development, management and utilization of energy
resources;
• To strengthen national, regional, and international cooperation in energy
generation and supply;
• To catalyse and accelerate sustainable economic growth of the nation
through energy generation and supply; and
• To strengthen institutional capacity.
• To ensure the sustainable management and utilization of mineral and
Petroleum resources;
• To monitor and assess geological hazards for proper planning and instituting
possible mitigation measures;
• To strengthen national, regional and international cooperation in mineral
resources management and development;
• To accelerate sustainable economic growth through mineral and Petroleum
resources exploitation; and
• To strengthen institutional capacity.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Commenced Construction works for the construction of a Battery Energy
Storage System at Kanengo in Lilongwe. The project is expected to stabilize the
grid once it is operational;
• Rehabilitated Tedzani Hydro power Plant which malfunctioned, causing a loss
of 31 megawatts from the grid . Works have been completed and the
generation machine is now back online.
• Secured financing amounting to USD350 Million to commence the construction
of the 358.5 MW Mpatamanga Hydropower Project. Preparatory works are now
underway, and the project has hired a contractor to implement the
Resettlement Action Plan, which is a critical step toward achieving financial
closure
• Advanced construction of the 20 MW Battery Energy Storage System, reaching
key implementation milestones toward grid stabilization and peak demand
management. The project is expected to be commissioned by March 2026.

=== Page 544 (pypdf) ===
544
• Electrified 389 sites against a target of electrifying 460 sites to the grid under the
Malawi Rural Electrification Programme (MAREP) Phase 9, with over 16,000
households and business entities connected to the grid;
• Successfully completed the Malawi Electricity Access Project in December
2025 which has helped to connect 180,752 households to the grid, surpassing
the target of 180,000. Additionally, the project has provided 255,742 households
with Solar Home Systems against an initial target of 210,000 and a revised target
of 262,542;
• Commenced the Accelerating Sustainable and Clean Energy Access
Transformation (ASCENT) Project. The project will connect 235,000 households
to the grid, provide 811,000 solar home systems, electrify 1,000 schools and 280
health facilities, and support 150, 000 households with clean cooking solutions
by 2030; and
• Commissioned Mwansambe Mini -Grid in Ntcheu connecting over 190
households to electricity.
• Completed all works on the Malawian Side of the Malawi -Mozambique
interconnector Project. Works on the Mozambique side are at an advanced
stage and commissioning is expected end February 2026; and
• Successfully secured financing from the European Union and AfDB amounting
to Euro 88 Million for the Eastern Backbone rehabilitation project.
• The review of the 2018 National Energy Policy is underway, with a first draft in
place;
• Commenced fuel security reforms, transition from G2G to Open Tendering
System of fuel procurement;
• The review of the 2017 IPP Framework is near completion;
• Launched the National Energy Compact to drive investment in key energy
sector priority areas; and
• Advanced bilateral engagements with Mozambique and Tanzania to secure
port access at Beira, Nacala, Dar es Salaam, Tanga and Mtwara for fuel
logistics.
• Successfully recommissioned the Kayerekera Uranium Mine under a new Mine
Development Agreement (MDA) between the Government and Lotus
Resources Limited;
• Advanced mineral exploration and data generation to inform investment
decisions. Reconnaissance and mapping surveys;
• continued research on agro -minerals to support local fertilizer production,
industrial minerals for cement and lime, and onshore oil and gas
reconnaissance in partnership with Press Trust Malawi and Africa Fortesa
Limited;
• Monitored geological hazards such as earthquakes and landslides to inform
land-use planning and community resilience;
• Successfully hosted the Second Malawi Mining Investment Forum, which
brought together over 500 local and international stakeholders from
government, industry, financial institutions, and development partners;
• Advanced the review of the 2013 Artisanal and Small-Scale Mining Policy;
• Implemented capacity-building programs for mining cooperatives, including
training and distribution of basic mining equipment to enhance productivity,
safety, and value addition;
• Reviewed its 2022 –2027 Strategic Plan to assess progress toward MW2063
targets. The review found that at least 50 percent of outcome and output
indicators are on track, though some key targets require additional resources.

=== Page 545 (pypdf) ===
545
• Continued its functional review to align its organizational structure with its core
functions and to ensure that the establishment warrant accurately reflects
these functions; and
• Developed a draft Communication Strategy designed to enhance
transparency, accountability, and stakeholder engagement.
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Foreign Currency Shortages: The unavailability of foreign currency significantly
hindered the importation of materials necessary for implementing various
programs and projects, including MAREP, MEAP, and ACRE.
• Non-Remittance of the Fuel Levy: Delays and non -remittance of the fuel levy
adversely impacted the effective implementation of the Malawi Rural
Electrification Programme (MAREP).
• Rising Production Costs: The devaluation of the Malawi Kwacha against major
currencies led to substantial price adjustments, increasing production costs
and straining project budgets.
• Fuel Scarcity: Persistent fuel shortages disrupted the implementation of critical
projects, such as the Malawi-Mozambique Interconnector.
• Political Unrest in Mozambique: Ongoing political unrest in Mozambique further
impeded progress on the Malawi -Mozambique Interconnector project,
delaying its implementation
• The Department of Mining faced challenges including inadequate Budget
which was also heavily cut during mid-year review.
• The Department of Mining also experienced delayed funding, devaluation of
the Malawi Kwacha leading to cost escalation, shortage of technical staff and
equipment,
• Persistent illegal mining, limited capacity to support artisanal miners; and
• Insufficient research funding.
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

ANTI-CORRUPTION BUREAU
Vote number: 510
Controlling Officer: The Director General
I. MISSION
To spearhead the fight against corruption in Malawi through prevention,
education and law enforcement.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To improve public service delivery.
• To strengthen enforcement of anti-corruption laws.
• To increase intolerance to corruption.
• To strengthen institutional capacity; and
• To strengthen planning, monitoring and evaluation.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• Reached out to approximately 9,955,094 people with anti -corruption
messages and reporting procedures through radio and television
programs, sports bonanzas, anti -corruption clinics, media workshops,
and corruption awarenesses;
• Produced and aired 94 radio programs on anti-corruption issues
• Produced and aired 10 Television programs on anti-corruption issues
• Commemorated the International Anti -Corruption Day (IAD) at Bingu
International Conference Centre in Lilongwe
• Commemorated African Anti-Corruption Day (AACD) in Mchinji District
• Conducted 459 community awareness meetings and workshops
• Conducted two (2) anti-corruption clinics
• Conducted five (5) sports bonanzas
• Produced and distributed 5,167 IEC materials
• Conducted a social corporate responsibility at Mchinji District Hospital
• Conducted 19 Trainer of Trainers (ToTs) for Civil Society organisations and
Anti-Corruption Champion Clubs
• Conducted 45 trainings in ethics and integrity for district council officials
• Trained 56 Institutional and Integrity Committees in ethics and integrity,
procurement monitoring, complaints handling and basic investigations
• Conducted one (1) media workshop in Liwonde, Machinga
• Conducted four (4) procurement audits.
• Conducted three (3) monitoring exercises to monitor implementation of
procurement audit recommendations
• Monitored two (2) National programs which included the Farm Input
Subsidy Program (FISP) and Mega Farms Initiative.
• Provided technical support to 27 institutions through development and
review of ethical tools
• Conducted two (2) risk assessments in institutions with high -risks of
corruption

=== Page 562 (pypdf) ===
562
• Conducted one (1) integrity testReceived and processed 422 corruption
complaints
• Investigated 497 corruption cases, of which 147 cases commenced in
the period
• Completed investigations of 134 cases
• 161 cases were in courts, of these cases, 17 commenced in the period
• Of the cases under prosecutions, 12 were completed. Of the completed
cases, 4 (33%) resulted in convictions.
• Produced annual report for the previous year.
• Recruited 8 additional staff and filled some vacant positions.
IV. PROGRAM ISSUES
• Inadequate Vehicles for operations
• Limited Office Space

LEGAL AID BUREAU
Vote Number: 520
Controlling Officer: Director of Legal Aid Bureau
I. MISSION
To be a lead actor in providing sustainable, accessible, ethical and high -
quality legal aid services to poor and vulnerable people in Malawi; and to
provide civic education and information about the law to the public.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To provide legal advice, legal assistance, or representation in any court,
tribunal or similar body or authority to the poor and vulnerable people in
Malawi.
• To provide civic education and information about the law to the public in
Malawi.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• The Bureau successfully procured five motor vehicles, comprising three
pool vehicles and two contractual vehicles, thereby strengthening
operational capacity across the country. Two of the five motor vehicles
were procured with financial support from the European Union.
• The Bureau opened two new district office in Nkhatabay, bringing the
total number of offices to 23 across 22 districts. This expansion has
improved accessibility to legal aid services in the newly established
districts.
• The Bureau conducted 22 legal awareness campaigns across various
districts, educating Malawians on available legal aid services and
increasing public awareness and sensitivity to legal rights.
• The Bureau conducted 18 legal aid clinics across prisons in Malawi, where
statements were recorded, bail applications processed and legal advice
provided, increasing accessibility to legal aid services.
• The Bureau successfully concluded 887 (479 civil, 408 criminal) cases in
court, and made 410 out of court settlements out of 8,486 cases handled
during the period.
• The Bureau paid annual practising licences for its 70 advocates on time,
which supported the continued handling of cases and improved service
delivery.
• The Bureau lobbied the Government for improved conditions of service
and recruitment of new officers within its current establishment warrant.
Following this, the Bureau recruited 38 officers and promoted 40
employees, therefore strengthening staff capacit y and reducing staff
turnover. The Bureau further initiated a functional review process with the

=== Page 570 (pypdf) ===
570
Department of Human Resource Management and Development
(DHRMD).
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
i. Inadequacy of resources which resulted in the following:
• Mobility challenges due to inadequate motor vehicles and an ageing
fleet, which affects timely service delivery.
• Insufficient human resources to effectively manage legal and
administrative requirements, with one lawyer handling an average of
over 500 cases.
• Inadequate office space to support the Bureau’s expansion drive.
• Shortage of furniture and equipment.
• Delays in funding and payment processing by the Accountant General’s
Office, and this disrupted implementation of key activities, causing some
service providers to terminate contracts or suspend services, and
increased operational costs due to hiring of motor vehicles.
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

OFFICE OF THE OMBUDSMAN
Vote number: 550
Controlling Officer: The Ombudsman
I. MISSION
To safeguard administrative justice and promote efficient and effective
public service delivery and good governance, through investigations of
maladministration and conduct advocacy on ethics.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• Increased accountability.
• Increased access to administrative justice.
• Strengthened institutional capacity
• Strengthened partnerships with stakeholders.
III. MAJOR ACHIEVEMENTS IN 2025/26
• The office resolved 155 one -off complaints and 191 complaints through
Mobile Accountability Clinics.
• Oriented 251 Hospital Ombudsmen across health facilities.
• Received 14,971 complaints and Resolved 10,029 complaints (67%)
through the Hospital Ombudsman Platform , (a slight increase from 2024
which was 14897) reflecting a 21% improvement from 2024.
• The office conducted a systemic investigation on the Department of
Immigration and Citizenship Services with the report set to be released by
March,2026 and on-going investigation on Mining.
• The office litigated 10 cases (4 new, 6 old), with 4 dismissed and 6 pending
rulings/judgments.
• Engaged 758 student teachers at Teacher Training Colleges to instil
principles of good governance in future educators
• With Funding from United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and
the European Union (EU) through Chilungamo II Programme the Office
conducted Mobile Accountability clinics (MAC) which is a proactive
intervention the OoO uses to take the office to the peop le reaching the
heart of communities in the rural areas or areas that are far from the main
offices to sensitize and empower power to identify and report acts of
maladministration causing injustices. The MAC took place in Chitipa,
Phalombe, Mulanje and Balaka.
• Attended AOMA Expo and IOI conferences
IV. PROGRAMME ISSUES
• Delays in responding to the correspondences of the office and outright
unresponsiveness among some MDAs. The OoO continued engaging MDAs
on the need to improve on the delays and unresponsiveness as well as

=== Page 578 (pypdf) ===
578
consequences of these vices on public administration and officials
themselves as provided in the Ombudsman Act.
• Non-compliance of the determinations (322 determinations are yet to be
complied on by MDAs)
• Limited knowledge of the role and functions of the Ombudsman by the
public
• Lack of office space
• Delays in effecting payments
V. PROGRAMME STRUCTURE

LAW COMMISSION
Vote number: 560
Controlling Officer: Law Commissioner
I. MISSION
To recommend laws that are contemporary and relevant in Mala wi and
to raise public awareness of the Laws of Malawi.
II. STRATEGIC OBJECTIVES
• To develop new laws in line with Malawi 2063;
• To review and make recommendations relating to the repeal and
amendment of the laws; and
• Promote awareness of the Laws of Malawi and the Constitution by the
public and by departments of the Government and other authorities or
bodies.
